- We need to determine the time **2,880,713 minutes earlier**.

A)4/7
B)2/3

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The triangle has vertices at \((0,0)\), \((4,0)\), and \((4,7)\).
- The triangular region lies in the first quadrant of the xy-plane.
- The inequality \(a - b > 0\) means that the x-coordinate of the point is greater than its y-coordinate (\(x > y\)).
- A point \((a, b)\) is selected randomly from the triangular region, meaning all points within the triangle are equally likely to be chosen.
- We need to calculate the probability that \(a - b > 0\), or equivalently, \(x > y\).

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Geometric approach
- The triangular region is defined by the vertices \((0,0)\), \((4,0)\), and \((4,7)\).
- The inequality \(x > y\) divides the triangle into two regions: one where \(x > y\) and one where \(x \leq y\).
- The line \(x = y\) intersects the triangle, and we can calculate the areas of the two resulting regions.
- The probability is the ratio of the area where \(x > y\) to the total area of the triangle.

   - The height of the smaller triangle is along the line \(x = 4\), from \((4,0)\) to \((4,4)\), with length 4.
   - Area = \(\frac{1}{2} \times \text{base} \times \text{height} = \frac{1}{2} \times 4 \times 4 = 8\).

Q: Mr. John used to purchase certain number of mangoes for $360 Since the price of mangoes is reduced by 10% he got 12 more mangoes today. Find the original price of 115 mangoes.
Options:
A)360
B)383
C)400
D)406
E)412
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Mr. John used to purchase mangoes for $360.
- The price of mangoes is reduced by 10%, and now he gets 12 more mangoes for the same $360.
- We need to find the original price of 115 mangoes.
- Assume the price per mango is uniform and consistent before and after the price reduction.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use algebra to calculate the original price per mango and then find the cost of 115 mangoes.
- Let the original price per mango be \( p \). The number of mangoes Mr. John could buy originally is \( \frac{360}{p} \).
- After the price reduction, the new price per mango is \( 0.9p \), and the number of mangoes he can now buy is \( \frac{360}{0.9p} \).
- The difference in the number of mangoes is 12:  
  \[
  \frac{360}{0.9p} - \frac{360}{p} = 12
  \]
- Solve for \( p \), then calculate the cost of 115 mangoes at the original price.

#### Method 2: Use proportional reasoning to find the original price per mango and then calculate the cost of 115 mangoes.
- The price reduction of 10% means the new price per mango is \( 90\% \) of the original price.
- The number of mangoes increases by 12, so the ratio of the original price to the new price can be used to find the original price per mango.
- Once the original price per mango is found, calculate the cost of 115 mangoes.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Algebraic approach
1. Start with the equation:  
   \[
   \frac{360}{0.9p} - \frac{360}{p} = 12
   \]
2. Factor out \( \frac{360}{p} \):  
   \[
   \frac{360}{p} \left( \frac{1}{0.9} - 1 \right) = 12
   \]
3. Simplify \( \frac{1}{0.9} - 1 \):  
   \[
   \frac{1}{0.9} = \frac{10}{9}, \quad \frac{10}{9} - 1 = \frac{1}{9}
   \]
4. Substitute \( \frac{1}{9} \) into the equation:  
   \[
   \frac{360}{p} \cdot \frac{1}{9} = 12
   \]
5. Solve for \( \frac{360}{p} \):  
   \[
   \frac{360}{p} = 12 \cdot 9 = 108
   \]
6. Solve for \( p \):  
   \[
   p = \frac{360}{108} = 3.33 \, (\text{original price per mango})
   \]
7. Calculate the cost of 115 mangoes:  
   \[
   \text{Cost} = 115 \cdot 3.33 = 383
   \]

#### Method 2: Proportional reasoning
1. Let the original price per mango be \( p \). After a 10% reduction, the new price per mango is \( 0.9p \).
2. The number of mangoes increases by 12, so the relationship between the original and new quantities is:  
   \[
   \frac{\text{Original quantity}}{\text{New quantity}} = \frac{p}{0.9p} = \frac{1}{0.9} = \frac{10}{9}
   \]
3. The original quantity is \( \frac{360}{p} \), and the new quantity is \( \frac{360}{0.9p} \). The difference is 12:  
   \[
   \frac{360}{0.9p} - \frac{360}{p} = 12
   \]
4. Solve for \( p \) using the same steps as Method 1 (steps 2–6). The original price per mango is \( 3.33 \).
5. Calculate the cost of 115 mangoes:  
   \[
   \text{Cost} = 115 \cdot 3.33 = 383
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 (algebraic approach) is more systematic and easier to follow for complex problems.
- Method 2 relies on proportional reasoning, which

Q: When m is divided by 9, the remainder is 8. When m is divided by 13, the remainder is 5. If 1 < m < 200, what is the greatest possible value of m?
Options:
A)47
B)65
C)103
D)117
E)161
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- When \( m \) is divided by 9, the remainder is 8. This means \( m \equiv 8 \pmod{9} \).
- When \( m \) is divided by 13, the remainder is 5. This means \( m \equiv 5 \pmod{13} \).
- We are tasked to find the greatest possible value of \( m \) such that \( 1 < m < 200 \).
- We assume \( m \) satisfies both modular congruences simultaneously.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use the Chinese Remainder Theorem (CRT).
- The CRT allows us to solve simultaneous modular congruences systematically. We find a solution to \( m \equiv 8 \pmod{9} \) and \( m \equiv 5 \pmod{13} \), then check for the largest \( m \) under 200.

#### Method 2: Use brute force by listing values.
- Generate all values of \( m \) that satisfy \( m \equiv 8 \pmod{9} \), then check which of these also satisfy \( m \equiv 5 \pmod{13} \). Finally, find the largest \( m \) under 200.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Use the Chinese Remainder Theorem.
1. Write the modular congruences:
   \[
   m \equiv 8 \pmod{9}
   \]
   \[
   m \equiv 5 \pmod{13}
   \]
2. The moduli \( 9 \) and \( 13 \) are coprime, so CRT applies. The solution is of the form:
   \[
   m = 9k + 8
   \]
   Substitute \( m = 9k + 8 \) into the second congruence:
   \[
   9k + 8 \equiv 5 \pmod{13}
   \]
3. Simplify:
   \[
   9k \equiv -3 \pmod{13}
   \]
   Since \( -3 \equiv 10 \pmod{13} \), we have:
   \[
   9k \equiv 10 \pmod{13}
   \]
4. Solve for \( k \) using modular inverses. The inverse of \( 9 \) modulo \( 13 \) is \( 3 \) (since \( 9 \cdot 3 \equiv 1 \pmod{13} \)):
   \[
   k \equiv 10 \cdot 3 \pmod{13}
   \]
   \[
   k \equiv 30 \pmod{13}
   \]
   \[
   k \equiv 4 \pmod{13}
   \]
5. Substitute \( k = 13j + 4 \) into \( m = 9k + 8 \):
   \[
   m = 9(13j + 4) + 8
   \]
   \[
   m = 117j + 44
   \]
6. Find values of \( m \) for \( 1 < m < 200 \):
   - For \( j = 0 \): \( m = 44 \)
   - For \( j = 1 \): \( m = 161 \)
   - For \( j = 2 \): \( m = 278 \) (too large, discard).

Thus, the possible values of \( m \) are \( 44 \) and \( 161 \). The greatest value is \( 161 \).

#### Method 2: Use brute force by listing values.
1. List values of \( m \) that satisfy \( m \equiv 8 \pmod{9} \):
   - \( m = 8, 17, 26, 35, 44, 53, 62, 71, 80, 89, 98, 107, 116, 125, 134, 143, 152, 161, 170, 179, 188, 197 \).
2. Check which of these also satisfy \( m \equiv 5 \pmod{13} \):
   - Test each value:
     - \( m = 44 \): \( 44 \div 13 = 3 \) remainder \( 5 \) (valid).
     - \( m = 161 \): \( 161 \div 13 = 12 \) remainder \( 5 \) (valid).
     - All other values fail the second

Q: The average (arithmetic mean) of all scores on a certain algebra test was 90. If the average of the 8 male students’ grades was 83, and the average of the female students’ grades was 92, how many female students took the test?
Options:
A)8
B)9
C)10
D)21
E)28
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The average score of all students on the test is 90.
- The average score of the 8 male students is 83.
- The average score of the female students is 92.
- We need to find the number of female students who took the test.
- Assume there are no other groups of students besides males and females.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Use the weighted average formula.**
- The overall average is a weighted average of the male and female averages.
- Let the number of female students be \( f \). The total number of students is \( 8 + f \).
- Use the formula for the weighted average:
  \[
  \text{Overall Average} = \frac{\text{Total Male Score} + \text{Total Female Score}}{\text{Total Number of Students}}
  \]
  Solve for \( f \).

**Method 2: Use the total scores approach.**
- Calculate the total score for the males and females separately.
- Let \( f \) be the number of female students. The total score for all students is:
  \[
  \text{Total Score} = (\text{Male Total Score}) + (\text{Female Total Score})
  \]
  Use the fact that the overall average is 90 to set up an equation and solve for \( f \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

---

#### **Method 1: Weighted Average Formula**
1. Let \( f \) be the number of female students.
2. The total number of students is \( 8 + f \).
3. The total score for the males is \( 8 \times 83 = 664 \).
4. The total score for the females is \( f \times 92 \).
5. The overall average is given as 90, so:
   \[
   \frac{664 + (f \times 92)}{8 + f} = 90
   \]
6. Multiply through by \( 8 + f \) to eliminate the denominator:
   \[
   664 + 92f = 90(8 + f)
   \]
7. Expand and simplify:
   \[
   664 + 92f = 720 + 90f
   \]
   \[
   92f - 90f = 720 - 664
   \]
   \[
   2f = 56
   \]
   \[
   f = 28
   \]

---

#### **Method 2: Total Scores Approach**
1. Let \( f \) be the number of female students.
2. The total number of students is \( 8 + f \).
3. The total score for all students is:
   \[
   \text{Total Score} = (\text{Number of Students}) \times (\text{Overall Average}) = (8 + f) \times 90
   \]
   \[
   \text{Total Score} = 720 + 90f
   \]
4. The total score for the males is \( 8 \times 83 = 664 \).
5. The total score for the females is \( f \times 92 \).
6. The total score for all students is also:
   \[
   \text{Total Score} = 664 + (f \times 92)
   \]
7. Set the two expressions for the total score equal to each other:
   \[
   720 + 90f = 664 + 92f
   \]
8. Simplify:
   \[
   720 - 664 = 92f - 90f
   \]
   \[
   56 = 2f
   \]
   \[
   f = 28
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** uses the weighted average formula directly, which is efficient and straightforward for problems involving averages.
- **Method 2** uses total scores, which is slightly more intuitive but involves more steps.
- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but Method 1 is slightly more efficient.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Using Method 1, we found that \( f = 28 \).

---

- Total score = \( 36 \times 90 = 3240 \).
- Male total score = \( 8 \times 83 =

- Their H.C.F. (Highest Common Factor) is 4.
- We are tasked with finding their L.C.M. (Least Common Multiple).
- Assumptions:

- The relationship between two numbers, their H.C.F., and their L.C.M. is:
  \[
  \text{H.C.F.} \times \text{L.C.M.} = \text{Product of the two numbers}.

4. Solve for \( \text{L.C.M.} \):

#### **Method 2: Calculate the numbers explicitly and find their L.C.M.**

3. The L.C.M. is the product of the highest powers of all prime factors:
   \[
   \text{L.C.M.} = 2^3 \times 3 \times 5 = 8 \times 3 \times 5 = 120.
   \]

- **Method 2** is more detailed and provides a direct understanding of the numbers and their L.C.M., but it involves more steps and is slightly less efficient.

  \]

  \[
  \text{L.C.M.} = \frac{480}{4} = 120.

Q: Find the largest number of four digits which is exactly divisible by 27,18,12,16
Options:
A)9906
B)9916
C)9936
D)9736
E)9746
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- We are tasked with finding the largest four-digit number that is exactly divisible by **27, 18, 12, and 16**.
- A number is "exactly divisible" by these values if it is divisible by their **Least Common Multiple (LCM)**.
- The largest four-digit number is **9999**.
- We assume all calculations are performed correctly and no numbers are skipped.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### Method 1: Calculate the LCM of the given numbers, then find the largest four-digit number divisible by the LCM.
1. Compute the LCM of 27, 18, 12, and 16.
2. Divide 9999 by the LCM and round down to the nearest integer.
3. Multiply the result by the LCM to get the largest four-digit number divisible by the LCM.

#### Method 2: Use prime factorization to find the LCM, then test divisibility starting from 9999 and working downward.
1. Perform prime factorization of 27, 18, 12, and 16.
2. Use the prime factors to compute the LCM.
3. Starting from 9999, check divisibility by the LCM until the largest divisible number is found.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Calculate the LCM and find the largest divisible number.**

1. **Find the LCM of 27, 18, 12, and 16:**
   - Prime factorization:
     - \( 27 = 3^3 \)
     - \( 18 = 2 \cdot 3^2 \)
     - \( 12 = 2^2 \cdot 3 \)
     - \( 16 = 2^4 \)
   - Take the highest powers of all prime factors:
     - \( 2^4 \) (from 16),
     - \( 3^3 \) (from 27).
   - LCM = \( 2^4 \cdot 3^3 = 16 \cdot 27 = 432 \).

2. **Find the largest four-digit number divisible by 432:**
   - Divide 9999 by 432:  
     \( 9999 \div 432 \approx 23.14 \).
   - Round down to the nearest integer: \( 23 \).
   - Multiply \( 23 \) by \( 432 \):  
     \( 23 \cdot 432 = 9936 \).

3. **Verify divisibility of 9936 by 27, 18, 12, and 16:**
   - \( 9936 \div 27 = 368 \) (integer, divisible).
   - \( 9936 \div 18 = 552 \) (integer, divisible).
   - \( 9936 \div 12 = 828 \) (integer, divisible).
   - \( 9936 \div 16 = 621 \) (integer, divisible).

   All checks confirm that 9936 is divisible by 27, 18, 12, and 16.

---

#### **Method 2: Use prime factorization and test divisibility.**

1. **Find the LCM using prime factorization (same as Method 1):**
   - LCM = \( 2^4 \cdot 3^3 = 432 \).

2. **Start from 9999 and test divisibility by 432:**
   - \( 9999 \div 432 \approx 23.14 \) (not divisible).
   - Test \( 9999 - 1 = 9998 \): \( 9998 \div 432 \neq \) integer.
   - Continue testing downward until \( 9936 \):
     - \( 9936 \div 432 = 23 \) (integer, divisible).

3. **Verify divisibility of 9936 by 27, 18, 12, and 16:**
   - Same checks as in Method 1 confirm that 9936 is divisible by all four numbers.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more efficient because it directly calculates the largest divisible number using division and multiplication, avoiding unnecessary testing.
- **Method 2** involves repeated testing, which is less efficient for large numbers.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

- Using **Method 1**, the largest four-digit number divisible by 27, 18, 12, and 16 is **9936**.

---

- The equation of the line is \( x = 2y + 5 \).
- Two points on the line are \( (m, n) \) and \( (m + 3, n + k) \).
- We need to find the value of \( k \), which represents the vertical change in \( y \)-coordinates between the two points.
- Since both points lie on the line, they must satisfy the equation \( x = 2y + 5 \).

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use the equation of the line to express \( n \) and \( n + k \) in terms of \( m \), then solve for \( k \).

#### Method 2: Use the slope of the line to relate the horizontal change (\( \Delta x = 3 \)) to the vertical change (\( \Delta y = k \)).

   \]
3. Solve for \( k \):
   \[
   k = \frac{1}{2} \cdot 3 = 1.
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 2** uses the slope of the line and is more intuitive, relying on the relationship between \( \Delta x \) and \( \Delta y \). It is faster but assumes the slope calculation is correct.
- Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is more general and reliable.

- From Method 1, \( k = 1 \).

C)8 : 9 : 8
D)7: 9 : 7
E)3 : 9 : 4
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and assumptions.
- Abhijit started the business with an investment of ₹70,000.
- Anuja joined after 6 months with ₹1,05,000.
- Sunil joined after another 6 months (i.e., 12 months after Abhijit started) with ₹1,40,000.
- The profit is to be distributed in the ratio of their investments multiplied by the time they were invested in the business.
- The total time considered is 2 years (24 months) from the start of the business.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Calculate the weighted contributions of each partner based on their investment and the time their money was invested.
- Multiply each partner's investment by the number of months their money was in the business.
- Use these weighted contributions to determine the ratio.

#### Method 2: Express the contributions as fractions of the total weighted investment.
- Calculate the total weighted investment by summing the contributions of all partners.
- Divide each partner's contribution by the total weighted investment to find their share of the profit.

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail.

1. **Abhijit's contribution**:
   - Abhijit invested ₹70,000 for the full 24 months.
   - Weighted contribution = ₹70,000 × 24 = ₹16,80,000.

2. **Anuja's contribution**:
   - Anuja invested ₹1,05,000 for 18 months (since she joined after 6 months).
   - Weighted contribution = ₹1,05,000 × 18 = ₹18,90,000.

3. **Sunil's contribution**:
   - Sunil invested ₹1,40,000 for 12 months (since he joined after 12 months).

   - Multiply by 2 to eliminate decimals:
     - Abhijit : Anuja : Sunil = 24 : 27 : 24
   - Simplify further:
     - Abhijit : Anuja : Sunil = 8 : 9 : 8.

#### Method 2:
1. **Calculate total weighted investment**:

2. **Calculate each partner's share as a fraction of the total**:

   - Anuja's share = ₹18,90,000 / ₹52,50,000 = 9/25.
   - Sunil's share = ₹16,80,000 / ₹52,50,000 = 8/25.

3. **Express the shares as a ratio**:
   - Abhijit : Anuja : Sunil = 8 : 9 : 8.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and directly calculates the weighted contributions, making it easier to understand and verify.
- **Method 2** involves additional steps to calculate fractions and convert them into a ratio, which is less efficient but still valid.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Method 1 is chosen for its simplicity and clarity.
- Final ratio: Abhijit : Anuja : Sunil = 8 : 9 : 8.

  - Sunil: ₹1,40,000 × 12 = ₹16,80,000.

A)10 % decrease
B)50% decrease
C)40% decrease
D)30% increase
E)100% increase

### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The rate of the chemical reaction \( R \) is:
  \[
  R \propto \frac{[B]}{[A]}
  \]
  where \([B]\) is the concentration of chemical B, and \([A]\) is the concentration of chemical A.
- If \([B]\) is increased by 100%, the new concentration of \([B]\) becomes \( 2[B] \).
- To keep \( R \) unchanged, \([A]\) must be adjusted accordingly.
- We are tasked with finding the percent change in \([A]\) required to maintain the same reaction rate.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use proportionality and algebra.
- Start with the proportionality equation:
  \[
  R = k \frac{[B]}{[A]}
  \]
  where \( k \) is the proportionality constant.
- Substitute the new \([B]\) and solve for the new \([A]\) to keep \( R \) constant.
- Calculate the percent change in \([A]\).

#### Method 2: Use ratios and percentages.
- Express the initial and final reaction rates as ratios of \([B]\) and \([A]\).
- Set the initial and final reaction rates equal to each other to find the required change in \([A]\).
- Use the relationship between the old and new \([A]\) to calculate the percent change.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Use proportionality and algebra.
1. Start with the equation for the reaction rate:
   \[
   R = k \frac{[B]}{[A]}
   \]
2. Let the initial concentrations be \([B] = B_0\) and \([A] = A_0\). Then:
   \[
   R = k \frac{B_0}{A_0}
   \]
3. When \([B]\) is increased by 100%, the new concentration becomes \( 2B_0 \). To keep \( R \) constant:
   \[
   R = k \frac{2B_0}{A_{\text{new}}}
   \]
4. Set the two expressions for \( R \) equal to each other:
   \[
   \frac{B_0}{A_0} = \frac{2B_0}{A_{\text{new}}}
   \]
5. Solve for \( A_{\text{new}} \):
   \[
   A_{\text{new}} = 2A_0
   \]
6. The percent change in \([A]\) is:
   \[
   \text{Percent change} = \frac{A_{\text{new}} - A_0}{A_{\text{new}}}

Q: The average (arithmetic mean) of the even integers from 0 to 40 inclusive is how much greater than the average (arithmetic mean) of the even integers from 0 to 20 inclusive?
Options:
A)10
B)20
C)5
D)30
E)15
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- We are tasked with finding how much greater the average of the even integers from 0 to 40 inclusive is compared to the average of the even integers from 0 to 20 inclusive.
- Even integers from 0 to 40 are: \(0, 2, 4, \dots, 40\).
- Even integers from 0 to 20 are: \(0, 2, 4, \dots, 20\).
- The formula for the arithmetic mean (average) of a set of numbers is:
  \[
  \text{Average} = \frac{\text{Sum of all numbers}}{\text{Number of numbers}}.
  \]
- We assume all calculations are exact and there are no missing numbers in the ranges.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of averages**
1. Calculate the sum of the even integers from 0 to 40 and divide by the count of these integers to find their average.
2. Similarly, calculate the sum of the even integers from 0 to 20 and divide by the count of these integers to find their average.
3. Subtract the two averages to find the difference.

#### **Method 2: Use properties of evenly spaced numbers**
1. Recognize that the even integers from 0 to \(n\) form an arithmetic sequence with a common difference of 2.
2. The average of an arithmetic sequence is equal to the average of its first and last terms.
3. Use this property to directly compute the averages for the two ranges (0 to 40 and 0 to 20) and subtract them.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of averages**
1. **Even integers from 0 to 40:**
   - Numbers: \(0, 2, 4, \dots, 40\).
   - Count of numbers: \( \frac{40 - 0}{2} + 1 = 21 \).
   - Sum of numbers: Use the formula for the sum of an arithmetic sequence:
     \[
     \text{Sum} = \frac{\text{Number of terms}}{2} \times (\text{First term} + \text{Last term}).
     \]
     Substituting:
     \[
     \text{Sum} = \frac{21}{2} \times (0 + 40) = 21 \times 20 = 420.
     \]
   - Average:
     \[
     \text{Average} = \frac{\text{Sum}}{\text{Count}} = \frac{420}{21} = 20.
     \]

2. **Even integers from 0 to 20:**
   - Numbers: \(0, 2, 4, \dots, 20\).
   - Count of numbers: \( \frac{20 - 0}{2} + 1 = 11 \).
   - Sum of numbers:
     \[
     \text{Sum} = \frac{11}{2} \times (0 + 20) = 11 \times 10 = 110.
     \]
   - Average:
     \[
     \text{Average} = \frac{\text{Sum}}{\text{Count}} = \frac{110}{11} = 10.
     \]

3. **Difference in averages:**
   \[
   20 - 10 = 10.
   \]

#### **Method 2: Use properties of evenly spaced numbers**
1. **Even integers from 0 to 40:**
   - First term = 0, Last term = 40.
   - Average = \(\frac{\text{First term} + \text{Last term}}{2}\):
     \[
     \text{Average} = \frac{0 + 40}{2} = 20.
     \]

2. **Even integers from 0 to 20:**
   - First term = 0, Last term = 20.
   - Average = \(\frac{\text{First term} + \text{Last term}}{2}\):
     \[
     \text{Average} = \frac{0 + 20}{2} = 10.
     \]

3. **Difference in averages:**
   \[
   20 - 10 = 10.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** involves more detailed calculations (sums and counts), which can be error-prone but is a good way to verify results.
- **Method 2** is faster and leverages the properties

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### **Method 1: Calculate the total area of the courtyard and divide by the area of one paving stone.**
- Find the area of the courtyard: \( \text{length} \times \text{width} \).
- Divide the total area of the courtyard by the area of one paving stone to determine the number of stones required.

#### **Method 2: Calculate the number of paving stones needed along the length and width separately, then multiply.**
- Determine how many paving stones fit along the length of the courtyard: \( \text{length} \div \text{stone length} \).
- Determine how many paving stones fit along the width of the courtyard: \( \text{width} \div \text{stone width} \).
- Multiply the number of stones along the length by the number along the width to find the total number of stones.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Total area divided by stone area**
1. Calculate the area of the courtyard:
   \[
   \text{Area of courtyard} = 30 \, \text{m} \times 18 \, \text{m} = 540 \, \text{m}^2
   \]
2. Calculate the area of one paving stone:
   \[
   \text{Area of one stone} = 2 \, \text{m} \times 2 \, \text{m} = 4 \, \text{m}^2
   \]
3. Divide the total area of the courtyard by the area of one paving stone:
   \[
   \text{Number of stones} = \frac{\text{Area of courtyard}}{\text{Area of one stone}} = \frac{540}{4} = 135
   \]
4. Check for potential pitfalls:
   - The calculation assumes the stones fit perfectly without gaps or cutting. Since the dimensions of the courtyard (30 m × 18 m) are divisible by the dimensions of the stones (2 m × 2 m), this assumption holds true.

#### **Method 2: Number of stones along length and width**
1. Calculate the number of stones needed along the length:
   \[
   \text{Stones along length} = \frac{\text{Length of courtyard}}{\text{Length of stone}} = \frac{30}{2} = 15
   \]
2. Calculate the number of stones needed along the width:
   \[
   \text{Stones along width} = \frac{\text{Width of courtyard}}{\text{Width of stone}} = \frac{18}{2} = 9
   \]
3. Multiply the number of stones along the length by the number along the width:
   \[
   \text{Total stones} = \text{Stones along length} \times \text{Stones along width} = 15 \times 9 = 135
   \]
4. Check for potential pitfalls:
   - The calculation assumes the stones fit perfectly along both dimensions. Since both the length (30 m) and width (18 m) are divisible by the stone dimensions (2 m), this assumption holds true.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and efficient, as it directly calculates the total area and divides by the area of one stone. It avoids intermediate steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2** involves more steps but provides a clear visualization of how the stones fit along the length and width. It is equally reliable but slightly less efficient due to the extra calculations.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.
- Solution:
  \[
  \text{Number of stones} = \frac{\text{Area of courtyard}}{\text{Area of one stone}} = \frac{540}{4} = 135
  \]

- The goal is to avoid being the one who shoots the pellet.
- You can choose to go first or second.

#### **Method 2: Simulate the sequence**

   - Assume the cylinder starts at a random position.
   - If you go first, you shoot at the first chamber. If it is empty, your friend shoots at the second chamber, and so on.
   - If you go second, your friend shoots at the first chamber. If it is empty, you shoot at the second chamber, and so on.

   - The pellets are consecutive, so the first empty chamber determines the sequence of turns leading to a pellet being shot.
   - Going first allows you to avoid the risk of shooting at a chamber that is guaranteed to contain a pellet.

❌ INCORRECT OR INVALID
Q: A shopkeeper cheats both his supplier and customer by using faulty weights. When he buys from the supplier, he takes 20% more than the indicated weight. When he sells to his customer, he gives the customer a weight such that 10% of that is added to the weight, the weight claimed by the trader is obtained. If he charges the cost price of the weight that he claims, find his profit percentage.
Options:
A)20%
B)32%
C)23.365%
D)22.109%
E)2%
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and assumptions.
- The shopkeeper cheats both his supplier and customer using faulty weights.
- **When buying from the supplier:** He takes 20% more than the indicated weight. This means if the supplier thinks he is selling 100 units, the shopkeeper actually receives 120 units.
- **When selling to the customer:** He gives the customer a weight such that 10% of that weight is added to obtain the claimed weight. This means if the shopkeeper claims to sell 100 units, he actually gives the customer only 90.91 units (since \( \text{actual weight} = \frac{\text{claimed weight}}{1.1} \)).
- The shopkeeper charges the cost price of the weight he claims to sell.
- We are tasked with finding the shopkeeper's profit percentage.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use a fixed weight (e.g., 100 units) as the base for calculations.
- Assume the shopkeeper buys 100 units from the supplier (indicated weight). He actually receives 120 units due to the 20% cheating.
- Assume the shopkeeper claims to sell 100 units to the customer. He actually gives the customer 90.91 units due to the 10% cheating.
- Calculate the cost price per unit and selling price per unit, then determine the profit percentage.

#### Method 2: Use algebraic expressions to generalize the problem.
- Let the indicated weight bought from the supplier be \( W \).
- Let the claimed weight sold to the customer be \( W \).
- Express the actual weight received from the supplier and the actual weight given to the customer in terms of \( W \).
- Calculate the profit percentage using the ratio of actual weight bought to actual weight sold.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### Method 1: Fixed weight approach.
1. **Buying from the supplier:**
   - Indicated weight = 100 units.
   - Actual weight received = \( 100 \times 1.2 = 120 \) units.

2. **Selling to the customer:**
   - Claimed weight = 100 units.
   - Actual weight given = \( \frac{100}{1.1} = 90.91 \) units.

3. **Profit calculation:**
   - Cost price per unit = \( \frac{\text{Total cost}}{\text{Actual weight received}} = \frac{100}{120} = 0.8333 \) (cost per unit).
   - Selling price per unit = \( \frac{\text{Total selling price}}{\text{Actual weight given}} = \frac{100}{90.91} = 1.1 \) (selling price per unit).
   - Profit per unit = \( 1.1 - 0.8333 = 0.2667 \).
   - Profit percentage = \( \frac{\text{Profit per unit}}{\text{Cost price per unit}} \times 100 = \frac{0.2667}{0.8333} \times 100 = 32% \).

#### Method 2: Algebraic approach.
1. **Buying from the supplier:**
   - Indicated weight = \( W \).
   - Actual weight received = \( W \times 1.2 \).

2. **Selling to the customer:**
   - Claimed weight = \( W \).
   - Actual weight given = \( \frac{W}{1.1} \).

3. **Profit calculation:**
   - Cost price per unit = \( \frac{\text{Total cost}}{\text{Actual weight received}} = \frac{W}{W \times 1.2} = \frac{1}{1.2} \).
   - Selling price per unit = \( \frac{\text{Total selling price}}{\text{Actual weight given}} = \frac{W}{\frac{W}{1.1}} = 1.1 \).
   - Profit per unit = \( 1.1 - \frac{1}{1.2} = 1.1 - 0.8333 = 0.2667 \).
   - Profit percentage = \( \frac{\text{Profit per unit}}{\text{Cost price per unit}} \times 100 = \frac{0.2667}{0.8333} \times 100 = 32% \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods yield the same result (32% profit percentage).
- Method 1 is more intuitive and easier to follow for fixed weights.
- Method 2 is more general and can be applied to any weight \( W \).

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Method 1 is chosen for simplicity and clarity.
- Final calculation confirms the profit percentage
Extracted Option:
None
Correct:
B

❌ INCORRECT OR INVALID
Q: Ernie invested Rs. 2000, with compounded interest annually. After 3 and half years the investment doubled. How many more years will it take before he earns another Rs. 4000 compound interest?
Options:
A)3.5 years
B)1.5 years
C)2.5 years
D)3 years
E)5 years
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and assumptions.
- Ernie invested Rs. 2000 with compounded interest annually.
- After 3.5 years, the investment doubled to Rs. 4000.
- We need to determine how many more years it will take for Ernie to earn another Rs. 4000 in compound interest, i.e., for the investment to grow from Rs. 4000 to Rs. 8000.
- Assumptions:
  - The interest rate remains constant throughout the investment period.
  - Compound interest is calculated annually.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use the doubling time principle.
- Since the investment doubled in 3.5 years, the doubling time is 3.5 years. Compound interest grows exponentially, so the investment will double again in another 3.5 years.

#### Method 2: Use the compound interest formula.
- The compound interest formula is:
  \[
  A = P \times (1 + r)^t
  \]
  where:
  - \(A\) is the final amount,
  - \(P\) is the principal,
  - \(r\) is the annual interest rate,
  - \(t\) is the time in years.
- First, calculate the annual interest rate \(r\) using the doubling time of 3.5 years. Then, calculate the time required for the investment to grow from Rs. 4000 to Rs. 8000.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Doubling time principle.
- The investment doubled from Rs. 2000 to Rs. 4000 in 3.5 years.
- Since compound interest grows exponentially, the investment will double again from Rs. 4000 to Rs. 8000 in the same amount of time: 3.5 years.
- Therefore, it will take 3.5 more years for Ernie to earn another Rs. 4000 in compound interest.

#### Method 2: Compound interest formula.
1. **Step 1: Calculate the annual interest rate \(r\).**
   - Using the formula \(A = P \times (1 + r)^t\), substitute:
     \[
     4000 = 2000 \times (1 + r)^{3.5}
     \]
   - Simplify:
     \[
     2 = (1 + r)^{3.5}
     \]
   - Take the 3.5th root of both sides:
     \[
     1 + r = 2^{1/3.5}
     \]
   - Approximate:
     \[
     1 + r \approx 1.20898
     \]
   - Subtract 1:
     \[
     r \approx 0.20898 \text{ or } 20.898\% \text{ annual interest rate.}
     \]

2. **Step 2: Calculate the time required for Rs. 4000 to grow to Rs. 8000.**
   - Using the formula \(A = P \times (1 + r)^t\), substitute:
     \[
     8000 = 4000 \times (1.20898)^t
     \]
   - Simplify:
     \[
     2 = (1.20898)^t
     \]
   - Take the logarithm of both sides:
     \[
     \log(2) = t \times \log(1.20898)
     \]
   - Solve for \(t\):
     \[
     t = \frac{\log(2)}{\log(1.20898)}
     \]
   - Approximate:
     \[
     t \approx \frac{0.30103}{0.08278} \approx 3.637 \text{ years.}
     \]
   - Therefore, it will take approximately 3.6 years for the investment to double again.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is simpler and relies on the doubling time principle, which is valid for compound interest with a constant rate. It directly concludes that the investment will double again in 3.5 years.
- **Method 2** uses the compound interest formula and logarithms to calculate the exact time, but the result (3.6 years) is very close to the doubling time of 3.5 years. This method is more complex and prone to rounding errors.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and reliability.
- Solution: The investment will double again in 3.5 years.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.
- Using Method 2, the calculated time (3.6 years) is very close to

D)160
E)180
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The length of the rectangular landscape is **4 times its breadth**.
- The area of the playground is **1200 square meters**, and it is **1/3rd of the total area** of the landscape.
- We need to find the **length of the landscape**.
- Assumptions:
  - The landscape is a perfect rectangle.
  - The total area of the landscape is evenly divisible by 3.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### Method 1: Use the relationship between the playground area and the total area.
1. The total area of the landscape is 3 times the playground area: \( \text{Total Area} = 3 \times 1200 = 3600 \, \text{m}^2 \).
2. Let the breadth of the landscape be \( b \). Then the length is \( 4b \), and the total area is \( \text{Length} \times \text{Breadth} = 4b \times b = 4b^2 \).
3. Solve for \( b \) using \( 4b^2 = 3600 \), then calculate the length \( 4b \).

#### Method 2: Use proportions and trial-and-error with the given options.
1. Assume the total area is \( 3600 \, \text{m}^2 \) (since the playground is 1/3rd of the total area).
2. For each option, calculate the breadth \( b \) using \( \text{Length} = 4b \), and check if \( \text{Length} \times \text{Breadth} = 3600 \).
3. The correct option will satisfy this condition.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic Solution**
1. Total area of the landscape:  
   \[
   \text{Total Area} = 3 \times 1200 = 3600 \, \text{m}^2.
   \]
2. Let the breadth of the landscape be \( b \). Then the length is \( 4b \), and the total area is:  
   \[
   \text{Area} = \text{Length} \times \text{Breadth} = 4b \times b = 4b^2.
   \]
3. Solve for \( b \):  
   \[
   4b^2 = 3600 \quad \Rightarrow \quad b^2 = \frac{3600}{4} = 900 \quad \Rightarrow \quad b = \sqrt{900} = 30.
   \]
4. Calculate the length:  
   \[
   \text{Length} = 4b = 4 \times 30 = 120.
   \]

#### **Method 2: Trial-and-Error with Options**

     \]
   - **Option B: Length = 140**  
     \[
     b = \frac{\text{Length}}{4} = \frac{140}{4} = 35, \quad \text{Area} = 140 \times 35 = 4900 \, (\neq 3600).
     \]
   - **Option C: Length = 120**  
     \[
     b = \frac{\text{Length}}{4} = \frac{120}{4} = 30, \quad \text{Area} = 120 \times 30 = 3600 \, (\text{Correct!}).
     \]
   - **Option D: Length = 160**

     \]

  \[
  (3x + 2x) + (4x - 2x) = 21
  \]

   \[
   (3x + 2x) + (4x - 2x) = 21
   \]

   \[
   5x + 2x = 21
   \]
   \[

   \]

   \[

Q: A worker is paid x rupees for painting every 30 metres of a wall and y rupees for painting every extra metre. During one week, he painted 30 metres on Monday, 30 metres on Tuesday, 32 metres on Wednesday, 32 metres on Thursday and 31 metres on Friday. What is his average daily earning rupees for the five day week?
Options:
A)x + (8/5)y
B)(5x + 9y)/5
C)10x + (8/5)y
D)None of these
E)x+y
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The worker is paid **x rupees** for painting every 30 metres of a wall.
- The worker is paid **y rupees** for painting every extra metre beyond 30 metres.
- The worker painted the following distances during the week:
  - Monday: 30 metres
  - Tuesday: 30 metres
  - Wednesday: 32 metres
  - Thursday: 32 metres
  - Friday: 31 metres
- We need to calculate the **average daily earning** for the five-day week.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Calculate total earnings for each day, sum them up, and divide by 5.
- For each day, calculate the earnings based on the formula:
  - If the worker paints exactly 30 metres, he earns **x rupees**.
  - If the worker paints more than 30 metres, he earns **x + (extra metres × y)**.
- Add up the earnings for all five days and divide by 5 to find the average daily earning.

#### Method 2: Use a general formula for total earnings over the week.
- Break the total earnings into two components:
  - Earnings for painting 30 metres each day: **5x** (since the worker painted 30 metres on all five days).
  - Earnings for extra metres: Calculate the total extra metres painted over the week and multiply by **y**.
- Divide the total earnings by 5 to find the average daily earning.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.
#### Method 1: Calculate daily earnings and average.
1. **Monday**: Painted 30 metres → Earnings = **x**.
2. **Tuesday**: Painted 30 metres → Earnings = **x**.
3. **Wednesday**: Painted 32 metres → Extra metres = 32 - 30 = 2 → Earnings = **x + 2y**.
4. **Thursday**: Painted 32 metres → Extra metres = 32 - 30 = 2 → Earnings = **x + 2y**.
5. **Friday**: Painted 31 metres → Extra metres = 31 - 30 = 1 → Earnings = **x + y**.

Total earnings for the week:
- Monday + Tuesday + Wednesday + Thursday + Friday = **x + x + (x + 2y) + (x + 2y) + (x + y)**.
- Simplify: **5x + 5y + 4y = 5x + 9y**.

Average daily earning:
- Divide total earnings by 5: **(5x + 9y) / 5**.

#### Method 2: Use a general formula for total earnings.
1. Total earnings for painting 30 metres each day:
   - The worker painted 30 metres on all five days → Total = **5x**.
2. Total extra metres painted:
   - Wednesday: 32 - 30 = 2 extra metres.
   - Thursday: 32 - 30 = 2 extra metres.
   - Friday: 31 - 30 = 1 extra metre.
   - Total extra metres = 2 + 2 + 1 = 5.
3. Earnings for extra metres:
   - Total extra metres = 5 → Total earnings for extra metres = **5y**.
4. Total earnings for the week:
   - Total = **5x + 5y**.
5. Average daily earning:
   - Divide total earnings by 5: **(5x + 9y) / 5**.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- Both methods yield the same result: **(5x + 9y) / 5**.
- Method 2 is slightly more efficient because it avoids calculating daily earnings individually and directly uses the total extra metres painted.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Using Method 2:
  - Total earnings = **5x + 9y**.
  - Average daily earning = **(5x + 9y) / 5**.

Q: A fair sided die labeled 1 to 6 is tossed two times. What is the probability the product of the two value is 1
Options:
A)1/6
B)7/36
C)1/36
D)9/36
E)11/36
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- A fair six-sided die is tossed twice.
- The die is labeled with numbers 1 through 6.
- The product of the two values from the tosses must equal 1.
- A fair die means each face has an equal probability of \( \frac{1}{6} \).
- We assume the tosses are independent, meaning the outcome of one toss does not affect the other.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Direct enumeration of outcomes
- List all possible outcomes of the two tosses (36 total combinations since \( 6 \times 6 = 36 \)).
- Identify the pairs of numbers whose product equals 1.
- Calculate the probability by dividing the number of favorable outcomes by the total number of outcomes.

#### Method 2: Mathematical reasoning
- Analyze the conditions under which the product of two numbers equals 1.
- Since the die is labeled 1 to 6, the only way the product can equal 1 is if both rolls result in the number 1.
- Use the probability of rolling a 1 on each toss and multiply them to find the overall probability.

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail.
#### Method 1: Direct enumeration of outcomes
1. The total number of outcomes is \( 6 \times 6 = 36 \), as each die has 6 faces and the tosses are independent.
2. To find favorable outcomes, check all pairs of numbers from the two tosses:
   - The product of two numbers equals 1 only if both numbers are 1.
   - The pair (1, 1) is the only favorable outcome.
3. There is exactly **1 favorable outcome**: (1, 1).
4. The probability is calculated as:
   \[
   P = \frac{\text{Number of favorable outcomes}}{\text{Total number of outcomes}} = \frac{1}{36}.
   \]

#### Method 2: Mathematical reasoning
1. The product of two numbers equals 1 only if both numbers are 1.
2. The probability of rolling a 1 on the first toss is \( \frac{1}{6} \).
3. The probability of rolling a 1 on the second toss is \( \frac{1}{6} \).
4. Since the tosses are independent, the overall probability is:
   \[
   P = \frac{1}{6} \times \frac{1}{6} = \frac{1}{36}.
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** involves explicitly listing all outcomes, which is clear but can be tedious for larger problems.
- **Method 2** uses mathematical reasoning, which is faster and more efficient for this problem.
- Both methods yield the same result, but Method 2 is more elegant and scalable.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Method 2 is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Using Method 2:
  \[
  P = \frac{1}{6} \times \frac{1}{6} = \frac{1}{36}.
  \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- The total number of outcomes is correctly calculated as \( 6 \times 6 = 36 \).
- The only pair of numbers whose product equals 1 is (1, 1), which is correctly identified.
- The probability calculation checks out: \( \frac{1}{36} \) is reasonable given the rarity of the event.

The answer is **C**.

**Method 2:**

   \]
3. Each box holds 2 dozens. Divide the total dozens by the capacity of each box:  
   \[

   \]
4. The result is 10 boxes.

- **Method 2** introduces an extra step (conversion to dozens), which is unnecessary and could lead to errors if the conversion is misunderstood.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

E)4 kmph
A_model:

- The man rows his boat **with the stream** at a speed of 20 km/h.
- The man rows his boat **against the stream** at a speed of 8 km/h.
- The man's **rate in still water** (his speed without the influence of the stream) is denoted as \( M \).
- The **rate of the stream** is denoted as \( S \).
- The relationship between these speeds is:
  - With the stream: \( M + S = 20 \)
  - Against the stream: \( M - S = 8 \)
- We assume the stream's speed \( S \) and the man's rate \( M \) are constant.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Solve the system of linear equations.
- Use the two equations \( M + S = 20 \) and \( M - S = 8 \) to solve for \( M \) (man's rate in still water) and \( S \) (stream's rate).

#### Method 2: Use the average speed formula.
- The man's rate in still water \( M \) is the average of his speeds with and against the stream:
  \[
  M = \frac{\text{Speed with stream} + \text{Speed against stream}}{2}
  \]
  This formula works because the stream's effect cancels out when averaging.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

   \[
   M + S = 20 \quad \text{(1)}
   \]
   \[
   M - S = 8 \quad \text{(2)}
   \]
2. Add equations (1) and (2) to eliminate \( S \):
   \[
   (M + S) + (M - S) = 20 + 8
   \]
   \[
   2M = 28
   \]
   \[
   M = 14
   \]
3. Substitute \( M = 14 \) into equation (1) to solve for \( S \):
   \[
   14 + S = 20
   \]
   \[
   S = 6
   \]
4. The man's rate in still water is \( M = 14 \) km/h, and the stream's rate is \( S = 6 \) km/h.

#### Method 2: Use the average speed formula.
1. Use the formula:
   \[
   M = \frac{\text{Speed with stream} + \text{Speed against stream}}{2}
   \]
2. Substitute the given speeds:
   \[
   M = \frac{20 + 8}{2}
   \]
   \[
   M = \frac{28}{2}
   \]
   \[
   M = 14
   \]
3. The man's rate in still water is \( M = 14 \) km/h.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** (solving equations) is more detailed and provides both the man's rate in still water (\( M \)) and the stream's rate (\( S \)). It is more versatile if additional information (like the stream's rate) is needed.
- **Method 2** (average speed formula) is faster and simpler but only works for finding \( M \) when the speeds with and against the stream are given.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- Since the problem only asks for the man's rate in still water (\( M \)), **Method 2** is more efficient.
- Using Method 2:
  \[
  M = \frac{20 + 8}{2} = 14
  \]

- Runner A finishes 12 meters ahead of runner B and 18 meters ahead of runner C.

**Method 2:** Assume the race length is \( L \), and use the time each runner takes to finish the race to derive equations for their speeds. Solve for \( L \) using the relationships between the runners' finishing positions.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

     \[

     \]

- A cyclist is moving along a road at a constant speed.
- Every 10 minutes, a bus overtakes the cyclist.
- Every 4 minutes, the cyclist meets an oncoming bus.
- All buses (overtaking and oncoming) are moving at a constant speed and are spaced at regular intervals.
- The time interval between consecutive buses (both overtaking and oncoming) is the same.
- The goal is to find the time interval between consecutive buses.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Relative speed approach
- Use the relative speed of the buses with respect to the cyclist to determine the time interval between consecutive buses.
- For overtaking buses, the relative speed is the difference between the bus speed and the cyclist's speed.
- For oncoming buses, the relative speed is the sum of the bus speed and the cyclist's speed.
- Relate the time intervals (10 minutes and 4 minutes) to the relative speeds to find the time interval between consecutive buses.

#### Method 2: Total distance approach
- Assume the buses are spaced at regular intervals along the road.
- Calculate the distance covered by the buses in the time it takes for the cyclist to encounter one overtaking bus and one oncoming bus.
- Use the relationship between the distances, speeds, and time intervals to find the time interval between consecutive buses.

   \]

7. The time interval between consecutive buses is the total time it takes for a bus to travel the distance between two consecutive buses:
   \[
   \text{Time interval} = \frac{\text{Distance between buses}}{\text{Bus speed}}
   \]
   Using the distance from the overtaking case:
   \[
   \text{Distance between buses} = 10 \times (v_b - v_c)
   \]
   Substituting \( v_b = \frac{7}{3}v_c \):
   \[
   \text{Distance between buses} = 10 \times \left(\frac{7}{3}v_c - v_c\right) = 10 \times \frac{4}{3}v_c = \frac{40}{3}v_c
   \]
   The bus speed is \( v_b = \frac{7}{3}v_c \), so:
   \[
   \text{Time interval} = \frac{\frac{40}{3}v_c}{\frac{7}{3}v_c} = \frac{40}{7} \, \text{minutes}.
   \]

Q: If a, b, and c are positive integers such that a is divisible by b, and c is divisible by a, which of the following is NOT necessarily an integer?
Options:
A)(a + c)/b
B)(c - a)/b
C)ca/b
D)(c + b)/a
E)cb/a
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- **Given:**
  - \( a, b, c \) are positive integers.
  - \( a \) is divisible by \( b \), meaning \( a = kb \) for some integer \( k \).
  - \( c \) is divisible by \( a \), meaning \( c = ma \) for some integer \( m \).
- **Assumptions:**
  - We are tasked with determining which of the given expressions is **not necessarily an integer**.
  - For an expression to be an integer, all terms in the numerator must be divisible by the denominator.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### **Method 1: Analyze each option algebraically.**
- Substitute \( a = kb \) and \( c = ma \) into each expression to check if it simplifies to an integer.
- For each option, verify whether the numerator is divisible by the denominator.

#### **Method 2: Use specific examples.**
- Assign specific values to \( a, b, c \) (e.g., \( a = 6, b = 2, c = 12 \)) and evaluate each option.
- Check if the result is an integer for all cases or if there is a counterexample.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
---

#### **Method 1: Analyze each option algebraically.**
1. **Option A: \( \frac{a + c}{b} \)**
   - Substitute \( a = kb \) and \( c = ma \):
     \[
     \frac{a + c}{b} = \frac{kb + ma}{b}.
     \]
   - Factor out \( b \) in the numerator:
     \[
     \frac{kb + ma}{b} = k + \frac{ma}{b}.
     \]
   - Since \( a = kb \), \( ma = m(kb) \), so \( \frac{ma}{b} = mk \), which is an integer.
   - Therefore, \( \frac{a + c}{b} \) is always an integer.

2. **Option B: \( \frac{c - a}{b} \)**
   - Substitute \( a = kb \) and \( c = ma \):
     \[
     \frac{c - a}{b} = \frac{ma - kb}{b}.
     \]
   - Factor out \( b \) in the numerator:
     \[
     \frac{ma - kb}{b} = m \cdot \frac{a}{b} - k.
     \]
   - Since \( a = kb \), \( \frac{a}{b} = k \), so:
     \[
     \frac{c - a}{b} = m \cdot k - k = k(m - 1),
     \]
     which is an integer.
   - Therefore, \( \frac{c - a}{b} \) is always an integer.

3. **Option C: \( \frac{ca}{b} \)**
   - Substitute \( a = kb \) and \( c = ma \):
     \[
     \frac{ca}{b} = \frac{(ma)a}{b} = \frac{ma^2}{b}.
     \]
   - Substitute \( a = kb \):
     \[
     \frac{ma^2}{b} = \frac{m(kb)^2}{b} = \frac{mk^2b^2}{b}.
     \]
   - Simplify:
     \[
     \frac{mk^2b^2}{b} = mk^2b,
     \]
     which is an integer.
   - Therefore, \( \frac{ca}{b} \) is always an integer.

4. **Option D: \( \frac{c + b}{a} \)**
   - Substitute \( a = kb \) and \( c = ma \):
     \[
     \frac{c + b}{a} = \frac{ma + b}{a}.
     \]
   - Split the fraction:
     \[
     \frac{c + b}{a} = m + \frac{b}{a}.
     \]
   - Since \( a = kb \), \( \frac{b}{a} = \frac{1}{k} \), which is **not necessarily an integer** unless \( k = 1 \).
   - Therefore, \( \frac{c + b}{a} \) is **not necessarily an integer**.

5. **Option E: \( \frac{cb}{a} \)**
   - Substitute \( a = kb \) and \( c = ma \):
     \[
     \frac{cb}{a} = \frac{(

- A's current age is \( x + 9 \) (since A is 9 years older than B).
- In 10 years, A's age will be \( x + 9 + 10 = x + 19 \).
- B's age 10 years ago was \( x - 10 \).
- According to the problem, A's age in 10 years (\( x + 19 \)) will be twice B's age 10 years ago (\( 2(x - 10) \)).
- Set up the equation: \( x + 19 = 2(x - 10) \).

#### Method 2: Use trial and error with the given options.
- Test each option for B's current age and check if it satisfies the conditions:
  - A is 9 years older than B.
  - A's age in 10 years is twice B's age 10 years ago.

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail.
#### Method 1: Solve using algebra.
1. Start with the equation: \( x + 19 = 2(x - 10) \).
2. Expand and simplify: \( x + 19 = 2x - 20 \).
3. Rearrange terms: \( 19 + 20 = 2x - x \).
4. Combine like terms: \( x = 39 \).
5. Verify:
   - If B is 39 years old, A is \( 39 + 9 = 48 \).
   - In 10 years, A will be \( 48 + 10 = 58 \).
   - B's age 10 years ago was \( 39 - 10 = 29 \).
   - Check if \( 58 = 2 \times 29 \): True.

#### Method 2: Solve using trial and error.
1. Test option A (\( x = 19 \)):
   - A's age: \( 19 + 9 = 28 \).
   - In 10 years, A's age: \( 28 + 10 = 38 \).
   - B's age 10 years ago: \( 19 - 10 = 9 \).
   - Check if \( 38 = 2 \times 9 \): False.
2. Test option B (\( x = 29 \)):
   - A's age: \( 29 + 9 = 38 \).
   - In 10 years, A's age: \( 38 + 10 = 48 \).
   - B's age 10 years ago: \( 29 - 10 = 19 \).
   - Check if \( 48 = 2 \times 19 \): False.
3. Test option C (\( x = 39 \)):
   - A's age: \( 39 + 9 = 48 \).
   - In 10 years, A's age: \( 48 + 10 = 58 \).
   - B's age 10 years ago: \( 39 - 10 = 29 \).
   - Check if \( 58 = 2 \times 29 \): True.
4. Test option D (\( x = 49 \)):
   - A's age: \( 49 + 9 = 58 \).
   - In 10 years, A's age: \( 58 + 10 = 68 \).
   - B's age 10 years ago: \( 49 - 10 = 39 \).
   - Check if \( 68 = 2 \times 39 \): False.
5. Test option E (\( x = 59 \)):
   - A's age: \( 59 + 9 = 68 \).
   - In 10 years, A's age: \( 68 + 10 = 78 \).
   - B's age 10 years ago: \( 59 - 10 = 49 \).
   - Check if \( 78 = 2 \times 49 \): False.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more systematic and efficient, as it uses algebra to directly solve for \( x \).
- **Method 2** requires testing multiple options, which is less efficient but can be useful for verification.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Method 1 is better due to its clarity

Q: A can contains a mixture of liquids A and B is the ratio 7:5. When 18 litres of mixture are drawn off and the can is filled with B, the ratio of A and B becomes 7:9. How many liter of liquid A was contained by the can initially?
Options:
A)32
B)52
C)62
D)42
E)48
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The can initially contains a mixture of liquids A and B in the ratio 7:5.
- 18 liters of the mixture are drawn off, and the can is refilled with liquid B.
- After refilling, the ratio of A to B becomes 7:9.
- We are tasked with finding the initial quantity of liquid A in the can.
- Assumptions:
  - The mixture is perfectly homogeneous, so the ratio of A to B in the 18 liters drawn off is also 7:5.
  - The total capacity of the can remains constant.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic Approach**
1. Let the total capacity of the can be \( x \) liters.
2. Initially, the quantities of A and B are \( \frac{7}{12}x \) and \( \frac{5}{12}x \), respectively.
3. When 18 liters are drawn off, the amounts of A and B removed are proportional to their initial ratio (7:5).
4. After refilling with 18 liters of B, the new ratio of A to B becomes 7:9.
5. Set up an equation based on the new ratio and solve for \( x \).

#### **Method 2: Logical Trial-and-Error**
1. Assume the total capacity of the can is one of the options provided (e.g., 32, 52, etc.).
2. Calculate the initial quantities of A and B based on the ratio 7:5.
3. Determine how much A and B are removed when 18 liters are drawn off.
4. Add 18 liters of B to the remaining quantities and check if the new ratio becomes 7:9.
5. Repeat for each option until the correct total capacity is found.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic Approach**
1. Let the total capacity of the can be \( x \) liters.
   - Initial quantity of A = \( \frac{7}{12}x \),
   - Initial quantity of B = \( \frac{5}{12}x \).

2. When 18 liters are drawn off:
   - Amount of A removed = \( \frac{7}{12} \times 18 = 10.5 \) liters,
   - Amount of B removed = \( \frac{5}{12} \times 18 = 7.5 \) liters.

3. Remaining quantities after removal:
   - Remaining A = \( \frac{7}{12}x - 10.5 \),
   - Remaining B = \( \frac{5}{12}x - 7.5 \).

4. After refilling with 18 liters of B:
   - New quantity of B = \( \frac{5}{12}x - 7.5 + 18 = \frac{5}{12}x + 10.5 \).

5. The new ratio of A to B is 7:9:
   \[
   \frac{\frac{7}{12}x - 10.5}{\frac{5}{12}x + 10.5} = \frac{7}{9}.
   \]

6. Cross-multiply to eliminate the fraction:
   \[
   9 \left( \frac{7}{12}x - 10.5 \right) = 7 \left( \frac{5}{12}x + 10.5 \right).
   \]

7. Expand both sides:
   \[
   9 \times \frac{7}{12}x - 94.5 = 7 \times \frac{5}{12}x + 73.5.
   \]

   Simplify:
   \[
   \frac{63}{12}x - 94.5 = \frac{35}{12}x + 73.5.
   \]

8. Combine like terms:
   \[
   \frac{63}{12}x - \frac{35}{12}x = 94.5 + 73.5.
   \]

   Simplify:
   \[
   \frac{28}{12}x = 168.
   \]

   Simplify further:
   \[
   \frac{7}{3}x = 168.
   \]

9. Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   x = 168 \times \frac{3}{7} = 72.
   \]

10. Initial quantity of A:
    \[
    \frac{7}{12}x = \frac{7}{12} \times 72 = 42.
    \]

---

Q: From a Group of 10 People, Including George and Nina, 3 people are to be selected at random to work on a certain project. What is the probability that 3 people selected will include George but not Nina
Options:
A)5/56
B)9/56
C)28/120
D)21/56
E)25/56
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are 10 people in total, including George and Nina.
- 3 people are to be selected at random.
- We are tasked with finding the probability that George is included in the selection, but Nina is not.
- Assumptions:
  - Each selection of 3 people is equally likely.
  - The order of selection does not matter.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Direct counting using combinations
1. Calculate the total number of ways to select 3 people from 10.
2. Calculate the number of ways to select 3 people such that George is included and Nina is excluded.
3. Divide the favorable outcomes by the total outcomes to find the probability.

#### Method 2: Complementary counting
1. Calculate the total number of ways to select 3 people from 10.
2. Subtract the cases where George is not included or Nina is included.
3. Divide the favorable outcomes by the total outcomes to find the probability.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### Method 1: Direct counting using combinations
1. **Total number of ways to select 3 people from 10:**
   \[
   \binom{10}{3} = \frac{10 \cdot 9 \cdot 8}{3 \cdot 2 \cdot 1} = 120
   \]

2. **Number of ways to select 3 people such that George is included and Nina is excluded:**
   - George is already included, so we need to select 2 more people from the remaining 8 people (excluding Nina).
   - The number of ways to do this is:
     \[
     \binom{8}{2} = \frac{8 \cdot 7}{2 \cdot 1} = 28
     \]

3. **Probability:**
   \[
   P = \frac{\text{Favorable outcomes}}{\text{Total outcomes}} = \frac{28}{120}
   \]

4. Simplify the fraction:
   \[
   \frac{28}{120} = \frac{7}{30}
   \]

#### Method 2: Complementary counting
1. **Total number of ways to select 3 people from 10:**
   \[
   \binom{10}{3} = 120
   \]

2. **Unfavorable cases:**
   - Case 1: George is not included.
     - If George is not included, we select all 3 people from the remaining 9 people:
       \[
       \binom{9}{3} = \frac{9 \cdot 8 \cdot 7}{3 \cdot 2 \cdot 1} = 84
       \]
   - Case 2: Nina is included.
     - If Nina is included, we select 2 more people from the remaining 8 people (including George):
       \[
       \binom{8}{2} = 28
       \]

3. **Favorable cases:**
   - Subtract the unfavorable cases from the total:
     \[
     \text{Favorable outcomes} = 120 - (84 + 28) = 8
     \]

4. **Probability:**
   \[
   P = \frac{\text{Favorable outcomes}}{\text{Total outcomes}} = \frac{8}{120}
   \]

5. Simplify the fraction:
   \[
   \frac{8}{120} = \frac{1}{15}
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and avoids the complexity of complementary counting. It directly calculates the favorable outcomes and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2** involves subtracting multiple cases, which increases the risk of miscounting or double-counting.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- Method 1 is better due to its clarity and simplicity.
- Using Method 1:
  \[
  P = \frac{28}{120} = \frac{7}{30}
  \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate the total number of ways to select 3 people:
  \[
  \binom{10}{3} = 120
  \]
- Recalculate the number of favorable outcomes:
  \[
  \binom{8}{2} = 28
  \]
- Recalculate the probability:
  \[
  P = \frac{28}{120} = \frac{7}{30}
  \

Options:

B)36
C)28
D)20
E)54
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The length of each train is 120 meters.
- The trains are running in opposite directions at the same speed.
- They cross each other in 8 seconds.
- We need to find the speed of each train in km/hr.
- Assumptions:
  - The trains are moving at constant speeds.
  - The total distance covered when they cross each other is the sum of their lengths (since they are moving in opposite directions).

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Use the relative speed formula.**
- When two objects move in opposite directions, their relative speed is the sum of their individual speeds.
- The total distance covered is the sum of the lengths of the two trains.
- Use the formula:  
  \[
  \text{Relative speed} = \frac{\text{Total distance}}{\text{Time taken}}
  \]
- Divide the relative speed by 2 to find the speed of each train.

**Method 2: Use unit conversion and proportional reasoning.**
- First, calculate the total distance covered (sum of train lengths).
- Then, calculate the relative speed in meters per second using the formula:  
  \[
  \text{Speed} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Time}}
  \]
- Convert the relative speed from meters per second to km/hr.
- Divide the relative speed by 2 to find the speed of each train.

   \]

   \[
   \text{Relative speed} = \frac{\text{Total distance}}{\text{Time taken}} = \frac{240}{8} = 30 \, \text{m/s}.
   \]

3. **Find the speed of each train.**  
   Since the relative speed is the sum of the speeds of both trains, and the trains are moving at the same speed:  
   \[
   \text{Speed of each train} = \frac{\text{Relative speed}}{2} = \frac{30}{2} = 15 \, \text{m/s}.
   \]

4. **Convert the speed to km/hr.**  
   To convert from meters per second to kilometers per hour, multiply by 3.6:  
   \[
   15 \, \text{m/s} \times 3.6 = 54 \, \text{km/hr}.
   \]

#### **Method 2: Use unit conversion and proportional reasoning.**
1. **Calculate the total distance covered.**  
   The total distance is the sum of the lengths of the two trains:  
   \[
   \text{Total distance} = 120 + 120 = 240 \, \text{meters}.
   \]

   Using the formula:  
   \[
   \text{Speed} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Time}} = \frac{240}{8} = 30 \, \text{m/s}.
   \]

3. **Convert the relative speed to km/hr.**  
   Multiply by 3.6 to convert from meters per second to kilometers per hour:  
   \[
   30 \, \text{m/s} \times 3.6 = 108 \, \text{km/hr}.
   \]

4. **Find the speed of each train.**  
   Since the relative speed is the sum of the speeds of both trains, and the trains are moving at the same speed:  
   \[
   \text{Speed of each train} = \frac{\text{Relative speed}}{2} = \frac{108}{2} = 54 \, \text{km/hr}.
   \]

- **Method 2** involves more intermediate steps but is useful for understanding the unit conversion process.

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- We are tasked with finding the sum of the **different positive prime factors** of 1080.
- A prime factor is a prime number that divides the given number without leaving a remainder.
- We assume 1080 is a positive integer and will use its prime factorization to identify its prime factors.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### Method 1: Prime factorization
1. Perform the prime factorization of 1080.
2. Identify the distinct prime factors.
3. Add the distinct prime factors together.

#### Method 2: Trial division
1. Use trial division to test divisibility of 1080 by successive prime numbers (2, 3, 5, etc.).
2. Record each prime factor only once.
3. Sum the distinct prime factors.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Prime factorization**
1. Start with 1080 and divide by the smallest prime number, 2:
   - \( 1080 \div 2 = 540 \)
   - \( 540 \div 2 = 270 \)
   - \( 270 \div 2 = 135 \)
   - 2 is no longer a factor of 135 (135 is odd).

2. Move to the next smallest prime, 3:
   - \( 135 \div 3 = 45 \)
   - \( 45 \div 3 = 15 \)
   - \( 15 \div 3 = 5 \)
   - 3 is no longer a factor of 5.

3. Move to the next smallest prime, 5:
   - \( 5 \div 5 = 1 \)
   - 5 is no longer a factor of 1.

4. The prime factorization of 1080 is:
   \[
   1080 = 2^3 \cdot 3^3 \cdot 5^1
   \]

5. The distinct prime factors are \( 2, 3, \) and \( 5 \).

6. Sum the distinct prime factors:
   \[
   2 + 3 + 5 = 10
   \]

---

#### **Method 2: Trial division**
1. Start with 1080 and test divisibility by successive primes:
   - \( 1080 \div 2 = 540 \): Record 2 as a prime factor.
   - \( 540 \div 2 = 270 \): 2 is already recorded.
   - \( 270 \div 2 = 135 \): 2 is already recorded.
   - 135 is not divisible by 2.

2. Test divisibility by 3:
   - \( 135 \div 3 = 45 \): Record 3 as a prime factor.
   - \( 45 \div 3 = 15 \): 3 is already recorded.
   - \( 15 \div 3 = 5 \): 3 is already recorded.
   - 5 is not divisible by 3.

3. Test divisibility by 5:
   - \( 5 \div 5 = 1 \): Record 5 as a prime factor.

4. The distinct prime factors are \( 2, 3, \) and \( 5 \).

5. Sum the distinct prime factors:
   \[
   2 + 3 + 5 = 10
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Prime factorization)** is systematic and provides a clear breakdown of the prime factors. It is efficient for small numbers like 1080.
- **Method 2 (Trial division)** is also effective but involves more repetitive testing, which can be tedious for larger numbers.
- Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is more straightforward and reliable for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

  2 + 3 + 5 = 10
  \]

Q: A case of 12 rolls of paper towels sells for $9. The cost of one roll sold individually is $1.What is the percent R of savings per roll for the 12-roll package over the cost of 12 rolls purchased individually?
Options:
A)9%
B)11%
C)15%
D)25%
E)90%
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- A case of 12 rolls of paper towels costs $9.
- The cost of one roll sold individually is $1.
- We are tasked with finding the percentage savings **per roll** when buying the 12-roll package compared to buying 12 rolls individually.
- Assumptions:
  - The cost of the 12-roll package is evenly distributed across all 12 rolls.
  - There are no additional costs, taxes, or discounts.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct calculation of savings per roll and percentage savings.**
1. Calculate the cost per roll in the 12-roll package: $9 ÷ 12.
2. Calculate the savings per roll compared to the individual price: $1 - (cost per roll in the package).
3. Divide the savings per roll by the individual price ($1) to find the percentage savings.

**Method 2: Total cost comparison and percentage savings.**
1. Calculate the total cost of 12 rolls purchased individually: $1 × 12.
2. Subtract the cost of the 12-roll package ($9) from the total cost of 12 rolls purchased individually to find the total savings.
3. Divide the total savings by the total cost of 12 rolls purchased individually to find the percentage savings, then confirm this percentage applies per roll.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of savings per roll and percentage savings**
1. **Cost per roll in the 12-roll package:**
   \[
   \text{Cost per roll} = \frac{\text{Total cost of package}}{\text{Number of rolls}} = \frac{9}{12} = 0.75 \, \text{dollars per roll}.
   \]

2. **Savings per roll:**
   \[
   \text{Savings per roll} = \text{Individual price per roll} - \text{Cost per roll in package} = 1 - 0.75 = 0.25 \, \text{dollars per roll}.
   \]

3. **Percentage savings per roll:**
   \[
   \text{Percentage savings} = \frac{\text{Savings per roll}}{\text{Individual price per roll}} \times 100 = \frac{0.25}{1} \times 100 = 25\%.
   \]

#### **Method 2: Total cost comparison and percentage savings**
1. **Total cost of 12 rolls purchased individually:**
   \[
   \text{Total cost (individual rolls)} = 1 \times 12 = 12 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]

2. **Total savings for the 12-roll package:**
   \[
   \text{Total savings} = \text{Total cost (individual rolls)} - \text{Cost of 12-roll package} = 12 - 9 = 3 \, \text{dollars}.
   \]

3. **Percentage savings for the 12-roll package:**
   \[
   \text{Percentage savings} = \frac{\text{Total savings}}{\text{Total cost (individual rolls)}} \times 100 = \frac{3}{12} \times 100 = 25\%.
   \]

4. **Confirm percentage savings per roll:**
   Since the savings are evenly distributed across all 12 rolls, the percentage savings per roll is the same as the total percentage savings: 25%.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and focuses on the savings per roll, which is the core of the problem. It involves fewer steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2** involves calculating total savings first and then confirming the percentage savings per roll. While it is slightly longer, it provides a broader perspective and serves as a good check for the result.

Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Cost per roll in the 12-roll package: $9 ÷ 12 = $0.75.
2. Savings per roll: $1 - $0.75 = $0.25.
3. Percentage savings: ($0.25 ÷ $1) × 100 = 25%.

  - Total cost of 12 rolls individually: $12.
  - Total savings: $12 - $9 = $

A)$1,430
B)$1,320
C)$1,290
D)$1,270
E)$1,150
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The tourist purchased $1,550 worth of traveler’s checks in $10 and $50 denominations.
- During the trip, the tourist cashed 7 checks in total.
- The number of $10 checks cashed was either one more or one less than the number of $50 checks cashed.
- The tourist lost all the remaining checks.
- We are tasked with finding the **minimum possible value of the checks that were lost**.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Systematic trial and error with equations
1. Let the number of $10 checks cashed be \( x \) and the number of $50 checks cashed be \( y \).
2. From the problem, \( x + y = 7 \) (since 7 checks were cashed).
3. Also, \( x = y + 1 \) or \( x = y - 1 \) (since the number of $10 checks cashed is one more or one less than the number of $50 checks cashed).
4. Solve for \( x \) and \( y \) in both cases, calculate the total value of the cashed checks, and subtract this from $1,550 to find the value of the lost checks.

#### Method 2: Direct algebraic approach
1. Let the total number of $10 checks purchased be \( a \) and the total number of $50 checks purchased be \( b \).

4. Subtract the value of the cashed checks from the total value to find the value of the lost checks.

#### Method 1: Systematic trial and error with equations

   - Case 1: \( x = y + 1 \)
     - Substitute \( x = y + 1 \) into \( x + y = 7 \):
       \[
       (y + 1) + y = 7 \implies 2y + 1 = 7 \implies 2y = 6 \implies y = 3.
       \]
       Thus, \( x = y + 1 = 4 \).
       - Number of $10 checks cashed: \( x = 4 \).
       - Number of $50 checks cashed: \( y = 3 \).
       - Total value of cashed checks: \( 10(4) + 50(3) = 40 + 150 = 190 \).

       \[
       (y - 1) + y = 7 \implies 2y - 1 = 7 \implies 2y = 8 \implies y = 4.
       \]
       Thus, \( x = y - 1 = 3 \).
       - Number of $10 checks cashed: \( x = 3 \).
       - Number of $50 checks cashed: \( y = 4 \).
       - Total value of cashed checks: \( 10(3) + 50(4) = 30 + 200 = 230 \).
       - Value of lost checks: \( 1,550 - 230 = 1,320 \).

2. Compare the two cases:
   - Case 1: Lost checks = $1,360.
   - Case 2: Lost checks = $1,320.
   - The **minimum possible value** of the lost checks is $1,320.
