❌ INCORRECT OR INVALID
Q: Albert invested Rs. 6500 in a scheme for 2 years at compound interest rate 6.5% p.a. How much amount will Albert get on maturity of the fixed deposit?
Options:
A)s.8829
B)s.7200
C)s.7200.5
D)s.4000
E)s.7372.46
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Principal (P) = Rs. 6500  
- Time (T) = 2 years  
- Annual compound interest rate (R) = 6.5%  
- Interest is compounded annually.  
- Formula for compound interest:  
  \[
  A = P \times (1 + \frac{R}{100})^T
  \]  
  where \(A\) is the maturity amount.  
- We assume no withdrawals or additional deposits during the 2 years.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct application of the compound interest formula.**  
- Use the formula \(A = P \times (1 + \frac{R}{100})^T\) to calculate the maturity amount.  
- Substitute the given values and compute step by step.

**Method 2: Break the calculation into yearly steps.**  
- Calculate the amount after the first year using simple interest for that year.  
- Use the resulting amount as the new principal for the second year and calculate the interest again.  
- Add the interest for both years to get the final maturity amount.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct application of the formula**
1. Write the formula:  
   \[
   A = P \times (1 + \frac{R}{100})^T
   \]
2. Substitute the given values:  
   \[
   A = 6500 \times (1 + \frac{6.5}{100})^2
   \]
3. Simplify the term inside the parentheses:  
   \[
   1 + \frac{6.5}{100} = 1 + 0.065 = 1.065
   \]
4. Raise \(1.065\) to the power of 2:  
   \[
   (1.065)^2 = 1.134225
   \]
5. Multiply by the principal:  
   \[
   A = 6500 \times 1.134225 = 7372.4625
   \]
6. Round to two decimal places (if necessary):  
   \[
   A = 7372.46
   \]

#### **Method 2: Break the calculation into yearly steps**
1. **First year:**  
   - Calculate the interest for the first year:  
     \[
     \text{Interest} = P \times \frac{R}{100} = 6500 \times \frac{6.5}{100} = 6500 \times 0.065 = 422.5
     \]
   - Add the interest to the principal to get the amount after the first year:  
     \[
     \text{Amount after 1 year} = 6500 + 422.5 = 6922.5
     \]

2. **Second year:**  
   - Use the new principal (\(6922.5\)) to calculate the interest for the second year:  
     \[
     \text{Interest} = 6922.5 \times \frac{6.5}{100} = 6922.5 \times 0.065 = 449.9625
     \]
   - Add the interest to the principal to get the maturity amount:  
     \[
     \text{Maturity Amount} = 6922.5 + 449.9625 = 7372.4625
     \]
3. Round to two decimal places (if necessary):  
   \[
   A = 7372.46
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it uses the compound interest formula in one step.  
- **Method 2** is more detailed and breaks the calculation into smaller steps, which can help with understanding but is more time-consuming.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better for clarity and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Using **Method 1**, the maturity amount is:  
  \[
  A = 6500 \times (1.065)^2 = 6500 \times 1.134225 = 7372.46
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using both methods: Both yield \(A = 7372.46\).  
- The result is reasonable, as the maturity amount is slightly higher than the principal due to compound interest over 2 years.

Q: The length of each side of square A is increased by 100 percent to make square B. If the length of the side of square B is increased by 75 percent to make square C, by what percent is the area of square C greater than the sum of the areas of squares A and B?
Options:
A)75%
B)80%
C)145%
D)150%
E)180%
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Square A has a side length \( s \).
- The side length of square A is increased by 100% to form square B. Thus, the side length of square B is \( 2s \).
- The side length of square B is increased by 75% to form square C. Thus, the side length of square C is \( 2s \times 1.75 = 3.5s \).
- The area of a square is given by \( \text{side length}^2 \).
- We are tasked with finding the percentage by which the area of square C exceeds the sum of the areas of squares A and B.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Direct calculation of areas.
1. Compute the areas of squares A, B, and C using their respective side lengths.
2. Add the areas of squares A and B.
3. Subtract the sum of the areas of A and B from the area of square C.
4. Divide the difference by the sum of the areas of A and B, and multiply by 100 to find the percentage increase.

#### Method 2: Use proportional reasoning.
1. Express the areas of squares A, B, and C in terms of the area of square A (\( s^2 \)).
2. Use the proportional relationships to calculate the percentage increase of square C's area over the sum of the areas of squares A and B.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.
#### Method 1: Direct calculation of areas.
1. **Area of square A**:  
   \[
   \text{Area of A} = s^2
   \]
2. **Area of square B**:  
   \[
   \text{Area of B} = (2s)^2 = 4s^2
   \]
3. **Area of square C**:  
   \[
   \text{Area of C} = (3.5s)^2 = 12.25s^2
   \]
4. **Sum of areas of A and B**:  
   \[
   \text{Sum of A and B} = s^2 + 4s^2 = 5s^2
   \]
5. **Difference between area of C and sum of A and B**:  
   \[
   \text{Difference} = 12.25s^2 - 5s^2 = 7.25s^2
   \]
6. **Percentage increase**:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage increase} = \frac{\text{Difference}}{\text{Sum of A and B}} \times 100 = \frac{7.25s^2}{5s^2} \times 100 = 145\%
   \]

#### Method 2: Use proportional reasoning.
1. **Express areas in terms of \( s^2 \)**:  
   - Area of A = \( s^2 \),
   - Area of B = \( 4s^2 \),
   - Area of C = \( 12.25s^2 \).
2. **Sum of areas of A and B**:  
   \[
   \text{Sum of A and B} = s^2 + 4s^2 = 5s^2
   \]
3. **Difference between area of C and sum of A and B**:  
   \[
   \text{Difference} = 12.25s^2 - 5s^2 = 7.25s^2
   \]
4. **Percentage increase**:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage increase} = \frac{\text{Difference}}{\text{Sum of A and B}} \times 100 = \frac{7.25s^2}{5s^2} \times 100 = 145\%
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods yield the same result.
- Method 1 involves direct calculations and is straightforward but requires more steps.
- Method 2 uses proportional reasoning, which is more efficient and avoids redundant calculations.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- Method 2 is better due to its efficiency and clarity.
- Using Method 2, the percentage increase is \( 145\% \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate the areas:
  - Area of A = \( s^2 \),
  - Area of B = \( 4s^2 \),
  - Area of C = \( 12.25s^2

A)40
B)10
C)50
D)30
E)20
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Initially, there is a group of boys and girls.
- 15 girls leave the group.
- After this, there are 2 boys for each remaining girl.
- Then, 45 boys leave the group.
- After this, there are 5 girls for each remaining boy.
- We need to find the number of girls in the beginning.
- Assume all numbers are whole numbers, and no one else joins or leaves the group.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use algebra to set up equations based on the given ratios and solve step by step.
- Let the initial number of girls be \( G \) and the initial number of boys be \( B \).
- After 15 girls leave, the number of girls becomes \( G - 15 \), and the ratio of boys to girls is \( 2:1 \).
- After 45 boys leave, the number of boys becomes \( B - 45 \), and the ratio of girls to boys is \( 5:1 \).
- Solve these equations to find \( G \).

#### Method 2: Use trial and error with the given options.
- Test each option for the initial number of girls (\( G \)) and calculate the corresponding number of boys (\( B \)) based on the ratios provided.

   - After 15 girls leave, the number of girls is \( G - 15 \).

     \[
     G - 10G = -375 + 15
     \]
     \[
     -9G = -360
     \]
     \[
     G = 40
     \]

6. **Find \( B \)**:
   - Substitute \( G = 40 \) into \( B = 2(G - 15) \):
     \[
     B = 2(40 - 15)
     \]
     \[
     B = 2(25) = 50
     \]

7. **Verify the solution**:
   - After 15 girls leave, there are \( G - 15 = 40 - 15 = 25 \) girls and \( B = 50 \) boys. The ratio is \( 50:25 = 2:1 \), which is correct.
   - After 45 boys leave, there are \( B - 45 = 50 - 45 = 5 \) boys and \( G - 15 = 25 \) girls. The ratio is \( 25:5 = 5:1 \), which is correct.

Q: A basket has 5 apples and 4 oranges. Three fruits are picked at random. The probability that at least 2 apples are picked is
Options:
A)25/42
B)25/40
C)25/49
D)25/47
E)25/44
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The basket contains 5 apples and 4 oranges, for a total of \( 5 + 4 = 9 \) fruits.
- Three fruits are picked at random.
- We are tasked with finding the probability that **at least 2 apples** are picked.
- Assumptions:
  - All fruits are equally likely to be picked.
  - The selection is without replacement.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Use the complement rule.
- Calculate the probability of the complement event (less than 2 apples picked) and subtract it from 1.
- Complement event includes:
  - Picking 0 apples.
  - Picking 1 apple.
- Use combinations to calculate probabilities for these cases.

#### Method 2: Direct calculation of favorable cases.
- Calculate the probabilities for the favorable cases:
  - Picking exactly 2 apples.
  - Picking exactly 3 apples.
- Add these probabilities together.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### Method 1: Complement Rule
1. **Total number of ways to pick 3 fruits from 9:**
   \[
   \binom{9}{3} = \frac{9 \cdot 8 \cdot 7}{3 \cdot 2 \cdot 1} = 84
   \]

2. **Case 1: Picking 0 apples (all oranges):**
   - Number of ways to pick 3 oranges from 4:
     \[
     \binom{4}{3} = \frac{4 \cdot 3 \cdot 2}{3 \cdot 2 \cdot 1} = 4
     \]
   - Probability:
     \[
     P(0 \text{ apples}) = \frac{\binom{4}{3}}{\binom{9}{3}} = \frac{4}{84} = \frac{1}{21}
     \]

3. **Case 2: Picking 1 apple (and 2 oranges):**
   - Number of ways to pick 1 apple from 5:
     \[
     \binom{5}{1} = 5
     \]
   - Number of ways to pick 2 oranges from 4:
     \[
     \binom{4}{2} = \frac{4 \cdot 3}{2 \cdot 1} = 6
     \]
   - Total ways to pick 1 apple and 2 oranges:
     \[
     \binom{5}{1} \cdot \binom{4}{2} = 5 \cdot 6 = 30
     \]
   - Probability:
     \[
     P(1 \text{ apple}) = \frac{30}{84} = \frac{5}{14}
     \]

4. **Complement probability (less than 2 apples):**
   \[
   P(\text{less than 2 apples}) = P(0 \text{ apples}) + P(1 \text{ apple}) = \frac{1}{21} + \frac{5}{14}
   \]
   - Convert to a common denominator:
     \[
     \frac{1}{21} = \frac{2}{42}, \quad \frac{5}{14} = \frac{15}{42}
     \]
     \[
     P(\text{less than 2 apples}) = \frac{2}{42} + \frac{15}{42} = \frac{17}{42}
     \]

5. **Probability of at least 2 apples:**
   \[
   P(\text{at least 2 apples}) = 1 - P(\text{less than 2 apples}) = 1 - \frac{17}{42} = \frac{25}{42}
   \]

#### Method 2: Direct Calculation
1. **Case 1: Picking exactly 2 apples (and 1 orange):**

     \[
     \binom{4}{1} = 4
     \]
   - Total ways to pick 2 apples and 1 orange:
     \[
     \binom{5}{2} \cdot \binom{4}{1} = 10 \cdot 4 = 40
     \]
   - Probability:
     \[

- He needs to select one trouser and one shirt.
- Each trouser can be paired with any of the shirts, and there are no restrictions on the combinations.
- We assume the selection process is independent, meaning the choice of trouser does not affect the choice of shirt.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Use the fundamental principle of counting. Multiply the number of trousers by the number of shirts to find the total number of combinations.  
**Method 2:** List all possible combinations explicitly (e.g., T1-S1, T1-S2, etc.) and count them manually.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.
#### **Method 1: Fundamental principle of counting**
1. The boy has 4 trousers and 7 shirts.
2. For each trouser, he can pair it with any of the 7 shirts.
3. Therefore, the total number of combinations is calculated as:  
   \[
   \text{Total combinations} = \text{Number of trousers} \times \text{Number of shirts} = 4 \times 7 = 28
   \]
4. This method is straightforward and avoids manual counting, reducing the chance of errors.

#### **Method 2: Listing all combinations**
1. List all possible combinations explicitly:
   - T1-S1, T1-S2, T1-S3, T1-S4, T1-S5, T1-S6, T1-S7 (7 combinations for T1)
   - T2-S1, T2-S2, T2-S3, T2-S4, T2-S5, T2-S6, T2-S7 (7 combinations for T2)

   - T4-S1, T4-S2, T4-S3, T4-S4, T4-S5, T4-S6, T4-S7 (7 combinations for T4)
2. Count the total number of combinations:  
   \[
   7 + 7 + 7 + 7 = 28
   \]
3. This method is reliable but tedious, especially for larger numbers.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more efficient and less prone to errors because it uses a simple mathematical formula. It is especially useful for larger numbers of trousers and shirts.
- **Method 2** is reliable but time-consuming and impractical for larger numbers. It is better suited for small-scale problems where manual verification is needed.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Using Method 1:  
   \[
   \text{Total combinations} = 4 \times 7 = 28
   \]

### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The area of a circle is added to its diameter.
- The circumference is subtracted from this total.
- The result is 10.
- We are tasked with finding the radius of the circle.
- Assume the circle's radius is \( r \), and use the following formulas:
  - Area of a circle: \( A = \pi r^2 \)
  - Diameter of a circle: \( D = 2r \)
  - Circumference of a circle: \( C = 2\pi r \)

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Solve algebraically using the given equation.
- Write the equation based on the problem statement:
  \[
  (\text{Area}) + (\text{Diameter}) - (\text{Circumference}) = 10
  \]
  Substitute the formulas for area, diameter, and circumference:
  \[
  \pi r^2 + 2r - 2\pi r = 10
  \]
  Simplify and solve for \( r \).

#### Method 2: Test the given options.
- Substitute each option for \( r \) into the equation:
  \[
  \pi r^2 + 2r - 2\pi r = 10
  \]
  Check which option satisfies the equation.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Solve algebraically.
1. Start with the equation:
   \[
   \pi r^2 + 2r - 2\pi r = 10
   \]
2. Combine like terms:
   \[
   \pi r^2 - 2\pi r + 2r = 10
   \]
   Factor out \( r \) from the linear terms:
   \[
   \pi r^2 + r(2 - 2\pi) = 10
   \]
3. Rearrange into standard quadratic form:
   \[
   \pi r^2 + (2 - 2\pi)r - 10 = 0
   \]
4. Solve this quadratic equation using the quadratic formula:
   \[
   r = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}
   \]
   Here:
   - \( a = \pi \)
   - \( b = 2 - 2\pi \)
   - \( c = -10 \)
   Substitute into the formula:
   \[
   r = \frac{-(2 - 2\pi) \pm \sqrt{(2 - 2\pi)^2 - 4(\pi)(-10)}}{2\pi}
   \]
5. Simplify the discriminant:
   \[
   (2 - 2\pi)^2 = 4 - 8\pi + 4\pi^2
   \]
   \[
   -4(\pi)(-10) = 40\pi
   \]
   \[
   \text{Discriminant} = 4 - 8\pi + 4\pi^2 + 40\pi = 4\pi^2 + 32\pi + 4
   \]
6. Substitute back and simplify further to find \( r \). This process is lengthy, so we will also test the options directly.

#### Method 2: Test the given options.
Substitute each option into the equation \( \pi r^2 + 2r - 2\pi r = 10 \):
1. **Option A: \( r = 2\pi \)**
   \[
   \pi (2\pi)^2 + 2(2\pi) - 2\pi(2\pi)
   \]
   \[
   = \pi (4\pi^2) + 4\pi - 4\pi^2
   \]
   \[
   = 4\pi^3 + 4\pi - 4\pi^2
   \]
   This does not simplify to 10.

2. **Option B: \( r = \pi \)**
   \[
   \pi (\pi)^2 + 2(\pi) - 2\pi(\pi)
   \]
   \[
   = \pi (\pi^2) + 2\pi - 2\pi^2
   \]
   \[
   = \pi^3 + 2\pi - 2\pi^2
   \]
   This does not simplify to 10.

3. **Option C: \( r = 2 \)**
   \[
   \pi (2)^2 + 2(2) - 2\pi(2)
   \]

❌ INCORRECT OR INVALID
Q: During the break of a football match, the coach will make 3 substitutions. If the team consists of 15 players among which there are 2 forwards, what is the probability that none of the forwards will be substituted?
Options:
A) 21/55
B) 18/44
C) 12/65
D) 28/44
E) 36/55
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The team consists of 15 players, including 2 forwards.
- The coach will make 3 substitutions during the break.
- We are tasked with finding the probability that **none of the forwards will be substituted**.
- Assumptions:
  - Substitutions are made randomly.
  - All players have an equal chance of being substituted.
  - The order of substitutions does not matter.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use combinations to calculate probabilities.
- Total players = 15, forwards = 2, non-forwards = 15 - 2 = 13.
- To ensure no forwards are substituted, all 3 substitutions must come from the 13 non-forwards.
- Use combinations to calculate:
  1. Total ways to choose 3 players from 15.
  2. Ways to choose 3 players from the 13 non-forwards.
  3. Probability = (Ways to choose 3 from non-forwards) / (Total ways to choose 3 from all players).

#### Method 2: Use complementary probability.
- Instead of directly calculating the probability of no forwards being substituted, calculate the probability of at least one forward being substituted and subtract it from 1.
- Use combinations to calculate:
  1. Total ways to choose 3 players from 15.
  2. Ways to choose at least 1 forward (e.g., 1 forward + 2 non-forwards, 2 forwards + 1 non-forward).
  3. Complementary probability = 1 - (Probability of at least 1 forward being substituted).

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Use combinations to calculate probabilities.
1. Total ways to choose 3 players from 15:
   \[
   \binom{15}{3} = \frac{15 \cdot 14 \cdot 13}{3 \cdot 2 \cdot 1} = 455
   \]
2. Ways to choose 3 players from the 13 non-forwards:
   \[
   \binom{13}{3} = \frac{13 \cdot 12 \cdot 11}{3 \cdot 2 \cdot 1} = 286
   \]
3. Probability that none of the forwards are substituted:
   \[
   P = \frac{\binom{13}{3}}{\binom{15}{3}} = \frac{286}{455}
   \]
4. Simplify the fraction:
   \[
   \frac{286}{455} = \frac{22}{35}
   \]

#### Method 2: Use complementary probability.
1. Total ways to choose 3 players from 15:
   \[
   \binom{15}{3} = 455
   \]
2. Ways to choose at least 1 forward:
   - Case 1: 1 forward and 2 non-forwards:
     \[
     \binom{2}{1} \cdot \binom{13}{2} = 2 \cdot \frac{13 \cdot 12}{2 \cdot 1} = 2 \cdot 78 = 156
     \]
   - Case 2: 2 forwards and 1 non-forward:
     \[
     \binom{2}{2} \cdot \binom{13}{1} = 1 \cdot 13 = 13
     \]
   - Total ways to choose at least 1 forward:
     \[
     156 + 13 = 169
     \]
3. Probability of at least 1 forward being substituted:
   \[
   P(\text{at least 1 forward}) = \frac{169}{455}
   \]
4. Complementary probability (none of the forwards substituted):
   \[
   P(\text{none of the forwards}) = 1 - P(\text{at least 1 forward}) = 1 - \frac{169}{455} = \frac{286}{455}
   \]
5. Simplify the fraction:
   \[
   \frac{286}{455} = \frac{22}{35}
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods yield the same result (\( \frac{286}{455} = \frac{22}{35} \)).
- Method 1 is more direct and involves fewer steps, making it more efficient.
- Method 2 is useful for understanding complementary probabilities but is more complex and prone to errors.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.

  SP2 = 1.25(0.8C) = 1.0C

Q: A train 125 m long passes a man, running at 7 km/hr in the same direction in which the train is going, in 10 seconds. The speed of the train is:
Options:
A)35 km/hr
B)50 km/hr
C)45 km/hr
D)52 km/hr
E)87 km/hr
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The train is 125 meters long.
- The man is running at a speed of 7 km/hr in the same direction as the train.
- The train passes the man completely in 10 seconds.
- We need to find the speed of the train in km/hr.
- Assumptions:
  - The train and the man are moving in the same direction.
  - The relative speed between the train and the man determines how quickly the train passes the man.
  - The conversion factor between meters per second (m/s) and kilometers per hour (km/hr) is \( 1 \, \text{m/s} = 3.6 \, \text{km/hr} \).

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Relative speed approach**
- The relative speed between the train and the man determines how quickly the train passes the man.
- Use the formula for relative speed: \( \text{Relative speed} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Time}} \).
- Add the man's speed to the relative speed to find the train's actual speed.

**Method 2: Convert speeds and solve directly**
- Convert the man's speed from km/hr to m/s.
- Use the formula \( \text{Relative speed} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Time}} \) to find the train's relative speed in m/s.
- Add the man's speed (in m/s) to the relative speed (in m/s), then convert the result back to km/hr.

---

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail.

#### **Method 1: Relative speed approach**
1. **Calculate the relative speed**:
   - Distance = 125 meters.
   - Time = 10 seconds.
   - Relative speed = \( \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Time}} = \frac{125}{10} = 12.5 \, \text{m/s} \).

2. **Convert the man's speed to m/s**:
   - Man's speed = 7 km/hr.
   - Convert to m/s: \( 7 \, \text{km/hr} \times \frac{1000}{3600} = \frac{7000}{3600} = 1.944 \, \text{m/s} \).

3. **Find the train's speed in m/s**:
   - Train's speed = Relative speed + Man's speed.
   - Train's speed = \( 12.5 + 1.944 = 14.444 \, \text{m/s} \).

4. **Convert the train's speed to km/hr**:
   - Train's speed = \( 14.444 \, \text{m/s} \times 3.6 = 51.998 \, \text{km/hr} \).

5. **Round the result**:
   - Train's speed ≈ 52 km/hr.

---

#### **Method 2: Convert speeds and solve directly**
1. **Convert the man's speed to m/s**:
   - Man's speed = 7 km/hr.
   - Convert to m/s: \( 7 \, \text{km/hr} \times \frac{1000}{3600} = \frac{7000}{3600} = 1.944 \, \text{m/s} \).

2. **Calculate the relative speed in m/s**:

5. **Round the result**:
   - Train's speed ≈ 52 km/hr.

❌ INCORRECT OR INVALID
Q: A monkey climbs 30 feet at the beginning of each hour and rests for a while when he slips back 20 feet before he again starts climbing in the beginning of the next hour. If he begins his ascent at 10.00 a.m., at what time will he first touch a flag at 120 feet from the ground?
Options:
A)4 p.m.
B)5 p.m.
C)6 p.m.
D)8 p.m.
E)None of these
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The monkey climbs **30 feet** at the beginning of each hour.
- After climbing, the monkey **slips back 20 feet** during the rest of the hour.
- The net gain per hour is therefore **10 feet** (30 feet climbed - 20 feet slipped).
- The monkey starts climbing at **10:00 a.m.**.
- The flag is located at **120 feet** from the ground.
- Once the monkey reaches or exceeds **120 feet**, it stops climbing and does not slip back.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Calculate the net gain per hour and determine how many hours are needed to reach 120 feet.
- Use the net gain of **10 feet per hour** to calculate how many hours are required to reach or exceed 120 feet.
- Account for the fact that in the final hour, the monkey climbs **30 feet** without slipping back.

#### Method 2: Track the monkey's progress hour by hour.
- Simulate the monkey's progress hour by hour, adding **30 feet** for climbing and subtracting **20 feet** for slipping back, until the monkey reaches or exceeds 120 feet.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Net gain calculation.
1. The monkey gains **10 feet per hour** due to climbing and slipping.
2. To reach **120 feet**, the monkey needs to climb for several hours:
   - After **11 hours**, the monkey will have gained **110 feet** (11 × 10 feet).
   - At the beginning of the **12th hour**, the monkey climbs **30 feet**, reaching **140 feet** (exceeding 120 feet).
3. The monkey reaches the flag during the **12th hour**, which starts at **9:00 p.m.**.

#### Method 2: Hour-by-hour simulation.
1. Start at **10:00 a.m.** with the monkey at **0 feet**.
2. Track progress hour by hour:
   - **10:00–11:00 a.m.**: Climbs 30 feet, slips back 20 feet → Net height = 10 feet.
   - **11:00–12:00 p.m.**: Climbs 30 feet, slips back 20 feet → Net height = 20 feet.
   - **12:00–1:00 p.m.**: Climbs 30 feet, slips back 20 feet → Net height = 30 feet.

     - **1:00–2:00 p.m.**: Net height = 40 feet.
     - **2:00–3:00 p.m.**: Net height = 50 feet.
     - **3:00–4:00 p.m.**: Net height = 60 feet.
     - **4:00–5:00 p.m.**: Net height = 70 feet.
     - **5:00–6:00 p.m.**: Net height = 80 feet.
     - **6:00–7:00 p.m.**: Net height = 90 feet.
     - **7:00–8:00 p.m.**: Net height = 100 feet.
     - **8:00–9:00 p.m.**: Net height = 110 feet.
   - At **9:00 p.m.**, the monkey climbs **30 feet** and reaches **140 feet**, exceeding 120 feet.
3. The monkey reaches the flag during the **12th hour**, which starts at **9:00 p.m.**.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient because it uses the net gain per hour to calculate the total time required, avoiding repetitive calculations.
- **Method 2** is more detailed but involves tracking progress hour by hour, which is slower and prone to errors in manual calculations.

Options:
A)150
B)200
C)121
D)140
E)150
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are 2 cricket teams, each consisting of 11 players.
- We need to select 10 players in total.
- No more than 1 player can be selected from each team.
- This means we can select **1 player from one team** and **9 players from the other team**.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Use combinations to calculate the number of ways to select players.
- From one team, select **1 player**: \( \binom{11}{1} \).
- From the other team, select **9 players**: \( \binom{11}{9} \).
- Multiply the results for the two teams, and account for the two possible configurations (Team A contributes 1 player, Team B contributes 9 players, and vice versa).

#### Method 2: Use symmetry and direct calculation.
- Recognize that the problem is symmetric: selecting 1 player from Team A and 9 from Team B is equivalent to selecting 1 player from Team B and 9 from Team A.
- Calculate the number of ways for one configuration (e.g., Team A contributes 1 player and Team B contributes 9 players), then double the result to account for symmetry.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.
#### Method 1: Use combinations.
1. Calculate the number of ways to select **1 player** from one team:
   \[
   \binom{11}{1} = 11
   \]
2. Calculate the number of ways to select **9 players** from the other team:
   \[
   \binom{11}{9} = \binom{11}{2} = \frac{11 \times 10}{2} = 55
   \]
3. Multiply the results for one configuration:
   \[
   11 \times 55 = 605
   \]
4. Since there are two configurations (Team A contributes 1 player and Team B contributes 9 players, and vice versa), multiply by 2:
   \[
   605 \times 2 = 1210
   \]

#### Method 2: Use symmetry and direct calculation.
1. Calculate the number of ways for one configuration (e.g., Team A contributes 1 player and Team B contributes 9 players):
   \[
   \binom{11}{1} \times \binom{11}{9} = 11 \times 55 = 605
   \]
2. Double the result to account for symmetry:
   \[
   605 \times 2 = 1210
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 explicitly accounts for the two configurations upfront, making it slightly clearer.
- Method 2 relies on recognizing symmetry, which might be less intuitive for some.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Method 1 is chosen for its clarity and explicit handling of configurations.
- Final calculation:
  \[
  \binom{11}{1} \times \binom{11}{9} \times 2 = 11 \times 55 \times 2 = 1210
  \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate:
  \[
  \binom{11}{1} = 11, \quad \binom{11}{9} = 55, \quad 11 \times 55 = 605, \quad 605 \times 2 = 1210
  \]
- The calculations are correct, and the result is reasonable.

  \]

Q: Sum of two numbers prime to each other is 15 and their L.C.M. is 56. What are the numbers?
Options:
A)9 and 8
B)5 and 10
C)7 and 9
D)7 and 8
E)10 and 8
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The sum of two numbers is 15.
- The least common multiple (L.C.M.) of the two numbers is 56.
- The two numbers are prime to each other, meaning their greatest common divisor (G.C.D.) is 1.
- We are tasked with finding the two numbers.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Use the relationship between the product of two numbers, their G.C.D., and their L.C.M.
- The product of two numbers \(a\) and \(b\) is given by:
  \[
  a \cdot b = \text{G.C.D.}(a, b) \cdot \text{L.C.M.}(a, b)
  \]
- Since the numbers are prime to each other, their G.C.D. is 1. Thus:
  \[
  a \cdot b = \text{L.C.M.}(a, b) = 56
  \]
- We also know \(a + b = 15\). Solve these two equations to find \(a\) and \(b\).

#### Method 2: Test the options provided.
- Each option gives a pair of numbers. For each pair:
  1. Check if their sum is 15.
  2. Check if their L.C.M. is 56.
  3. Check if the numbers are prime to each other (G.C.D. = 1).
- The pair that satisfies all conditions is the solution.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Use the product and sum equations.
1. From the given information:
   \[
   a + b = 15 \quad \text{and} \quad a \cdot b = 56
   \]
2. Rewrite \(b\) in terms of \(a\): \(b = 15 - a\).
3. Substitute \(b = 15 - a\) into \(a \cdot b = 56\):
   \[
   a \cdot (15 - a) = 56
   \]
   Expand and rearrange:
   \[
   a^2 - 15a + 56 = 0
   \]
4. Solve the quadratic equation using the quadratic formula:
   \[
   a = \frac{-(-15) \pm \sqrt{(-15)^2 - 4(1)(56)}}{2(1)}
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   a = \frac{15 \pm \sqrt{225 - 224}}{2}
   \]
   \[
   a = \frac{15 \pm \sqrt{1}}{2}
   \]
   \[
   a = \frac{15 + 1}{2} = 8 \quad \text{or} \quad a = \frac{15 - 1}{2} = 7
   \]
5. Thus, the two numbers are \(a = 8\) and \(b = 7\).

#### Method 2: Test the options.
- **Option A: 9 and 8**

  - Sum: \(10 + 8 = 18 \neq 15\). Discard.

❌ INCORRECT OR INVALID
Q: Two trains, 250 metres and 150 metres long respectively, are running on parallel lines. If they are running in the same directions, the faster train crosses the slower train in 40 seconds. If they are moving in the opposite direction they pass each other in eight seconds. What is the speed of the slower train?
Options:
A)108kmph
B)82kmph
C)92 kmph
D)72 kmph
E)87 kmph
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and assumptions.
- Train 1 is 250 meters long, and Train 2 is 150 meters long.
- When running in the same direction, the faster train crosses the slower train in **40 seconds**.
- When running in opposite directions, the trains pass each other in **8 seconds**.
- We need to find the speed of the slower train.
- Assume both trains maintain constant speeds throughout.
- Speeds are to be calculated in **km/h**.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use relative speed formulas for both cases (same direction and opposite direction).
- In the same direction, relative speed = difference in speeds.
- In opposite directions, relative speed = sum of speeds.
- Use the distances covered and times given to set up equations and solve for the slower train's speed.

#### Method 2: Solve using the concept of total distance covered and relative speed.
- Total distance covered = sum of train lengths (250 + 150 = 400 meters).
- Use the relative speed formulas for both cases to calculate the slower train's speed step by step.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### Method 1: Relative speed formulas.
1. **Same direction case**:
   - Relative speed = difference in speeds.
   - Distance covered = sum of train lengths = 250 + 150 = 400 meters.
   - Time taken = 40 seconds.
   - Relative speed in m/s = distance / time = 400 / 40 = 10 m/s.

2. **Opposite direction case**:
   - Relative speed = sum of speeds.
   - Distance covered = sum of train lengths = 400 meters.
   - Time taken = 8 seconds.
   - Relative speed in m/s = distance / time = 400 / 8 = 50 m/s.

3. Convert relative speeds to km/h:
   - Relative speed in same direction = 10 m/s × 3.6 = 36 km/h.
   - Relative speed in opposite direction = 50 m/s × 3.6 = 180 km/h.

4. Let the speed of the slower train be \( S \) km/h and the faster train be \( F \) km/h.
   - From the same direction case: \( F - S = 36 \).
   - From the opposite direction case: \( F + S = 180 \).

5. Solve the system of equations:
   - \( F - S = 36 \)
   - \( F + S = 180 \)
   - Add the equations: \( 2F = 216 \) → \( F = 108 \) km/h.
   - Substitute \( F = 108 \) into \( F - S = 36 \): \( 108 - S = 36 \) → \( S = 72 \) km/h.

#### Method 2: Total distance and relative speed.
1. **Same direction case**:
   - Distance covered = sum of train lengths = 400 meters.
   - Time taken = 40 seconds.
   - Relative speed in m/s = distance / time = 400 / 40 = 10 m/s.

2. **Opposite direction case**:
   - Distance covered = sum of train lengths = 400 meters.
   - Time taken = 8 seconds.
   - Relative speed in m/s = distance / time = 400 / 8 = 50 m/s.

3. Convert relative speeds to km/h:
   - Relative speed in same direction = 10 m/s × 3.6 = 36 km/h.
   - Relative speed in opposite direction = 50 m/s × 3.6 = 180 km/h.

4. Let the speed of the slower train be \( S \) km/h and the faster train be \( F \) km/h.
   - From the same direction case: \( F - S = 36 \).
   - From the opposite direction case: \( F + S = 180 \).

5. Solve the system of equations:
   - \( F - S = 36 \)
   - \( F + S = 180 \)
   - Add the equations: \( 2F = 216 \) → \( F = 108 \) km/h.
   - Substitute \( F = 108 \) into \( F - S = 36 \): \( 108 - S = 36 \) → \( S = 72 \) km/h.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods rely on the same relative speed formulas and yield the same result.

Q: A boat ma goes 2km against the current of the stream in 1 hr and goes 1 km along the current in 10 min. how long will it take to go 12 km in stationary water
Options:
A)40 min
B)3hr
C)1hr 15 min
D)1hr 30 min
E)4 hr
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The boat travels **2 km against the current in 1 hour**. This gives the effective speed of the boat against the current as \( v_{\text{against}} = 2 \, \text{km/hr} \).
- The boat travels **1 km along the current in 10 minutes**. Converting 10 minutes to hours (\( 10 \, \text{min} = \frac{10}{60} = \frac{1}{6} \, \text{hr} \)), the effective speed of the boat along the current is \( v_{\text{along}} = \frac{1}{\frac{1}{6}} = 6 \, \text{km/hr} \).
- We are tasked with finding the time it takes for the boat to travel **12 km in stationary water** (i.e., no current).
- Assumptions:
  - The boat's speed in stationary water is constant.
  - The current's speed is uniform and does not vary.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### Method 1: Use the effective speeds to calculate the boat's speed in stationary water.
- The effective speed of the boat against the current is \( v_{\text{boat}} - v_{\text{current}} = 2 \, \text{km/hr} \).
- The effective speed of the boat along the current is \( v_{\text{boat}} + v_{\text{current}} = 6 \, \text{km/hr} \).
- Solve these two equations simultaneously to find \( v_{\text{boat}} \), the boat's speed in stationary water.
- Use \( v_{\text{boat}} \) to calculate the time to travel 12 km in stationary water.

#### Method 2: Use the average of the effective speeds.
- The boat's speed in stationary water is the average of its effective speeds against and along the current:
  \[
  v_{\text{boat}} = \frac{v_{\text{against}} + v_{\text{along}}}{2}
  \]
- Use this average speed to calculate the time to travel 12 km in stationary water.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### Method 1: Solve using simultaneous equations.
1. Let \( v_{\text{boat}} \) be the boat's speed in stationary water, and \( v_{\text{current}} \) be the speed of the current.
2. From the problem:
   \[
   v_{\text{boat}} - v_{\text{current}} = 2 \quad \text{(against the current)}
   \]
   \[
   v_{\text{boat}} + v_{\text{current}} = 6 \quad \text{(along the current)}
   \]
3. Add the two equations:
   \[
   (v_{\text{boat}} - v_{\text{current}}) + (v_{\text{boat}} + v_{\text{current}}) = 2 + 6
   \]
   \[
   2v_{\text{boat}} = 8 \quad \Rightarrow \quad v_{\text{boat}} = 4 \, \text{km/hr}
   \]
4. Use \( v_{\text{boat}} = 4 \, \text{km/hr} \) to calculate the time to travel 12 km in stationary water:
   \[
   \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Speed}} = \frac{12}{4} = 3 \, \text{hours}.
   \]

#### Method 2: Use the average of the effective speeds.
1. The effective speed against the current is \( v_{\text{against}} = 2 \, \text{km/hr} \), and the effective speed along the current is \( v_{\text{along}} = 6 \, \text{km/hr} \).
2. The boat's speed in stationary water is the average of these two speeds:
   \[
   v_{\text{boat}} = \frac{v_{\text{against}} + v_{\text{along}}}{2} = \frac{2 + 6}{2} = 4 \, \text{km/hr}.
   \]
3. Use \( v_{\text{boat}} = 4 \, \text{km/hr} \) to calculate the time to travel 12 km in stationary water:
   \[
   \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Speed}} = \frac{12}{4} = 3 \, \text{hours}.
   \]

---

- Both methods yield the same result (\

❌ INCORRECT OR INVALID
Q: What will be the day of the week on 15th August,2010 ?
Options:
A)Sunday
B)Monday
C)Tuesday
D)Friday
E)None of these
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- We are tasked with finding the day of the week for 15th August 2010.
- The Gregorian calendar is used.
- Days of the week repeat in a 7-day cycle: Sunday, Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, Saturday.
- We assume no errors in the calendar system (e.g., no skipped days).

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Use Zeller's Congruence Formula**  
Zeller's Congruence is a mathematical formula to calculate the day of the week for any given date. The formula is:  
\[ h = \left( q + \left\lfloor \frac{13(m+1)}{5} \right\rfloor + K + \left\lfloor \frac{K}{4} \right\rfloor + \left\lfloor \frac{J}{4} \right\rfloor - 2J \right) \mod 7 \]  
Where:  
- \( h \) is the day of the week (0 = Saturday, 1 = Sunday, ..., 6 = Friday).  
- \( q \) is the day of the month (15 in this case).  
- \( m \) is the month (August = 8, but for Zeller's formula, January and February are treated as months 13 and 14 of the previous year).  
- \( K \) is the year of the century (last two digits of the year, 2010 → 10).  
- \( J \) is the century (first two digits of the year, 2010 → 20).  

**Method 2: Count days from a known reference date**  
We can use a known reference date (e.g., 1st January 1900 was a Monday) and count the total number of days from that date to 15th August 2010. Then, divide the total days by 7 to find the remainder, which gives the day of the week.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Zeller's Congruence**
1. The date is 15th August 2010.  
   - \( q = 15 \) (day of the month).  
   - \( m = 8 \) (August).  
   - \( K = 10 \) (last two digits of 2010).  
   - \( J = 20 \) (first two digits of 2010).  

2. Plug values into the formula:  
   \[
   h = \left( 15 + \left\lfloor \frac{13(8+1)}{5} \right\rfloor + 10 + \left\lfloor \frac{10}{4} \right\rfloor + \left\lfloor \frac{20}{4} \right\rfloor - 2(20) \right) \mod 7
   \]

3. Simplify step by step:  
   - \( \left\lfloor \frac{13(8+1)}{5} \right\rfloor = \left\lfloor \frac{13 \cdot 9}{5} \right\rfloor = \left\lfloor 23.4 \right\rfloor = 23 \).  
   - \( \left\lfloor \frac{10}{4} \right\rfloor = \left\lfloor 2.5 \right\rfloor = 2 \).  
   - \( \left\lfloor \frac{20}{4} \right\rfloor = \left\lfloor 5 \right\rfloor = 5 \).  

   Substituting these values:  
   \[
   h = \left( 15 + 23 + 10 + 2 + 5 - 40 \right) \mod 7
   \]

4. Simplify further:  
   \[
   h = \left( 15 + 23 + 10 + 2 + 5 - 40 \right) = 15.
   \]
   \[
   h = 15 \mod 7 = 1.
   \]

5. Interpret the result:  
   - \( h = 1 \) corresponds to Sunday.  

#### **Method 2: Count days from a known reference date**
1. Start with 1st January 1900, which was a Monday.  
2. Count the total number of days from 1st January 1900 to 15th August 2010:  
   - From 1900 to 2000 (a leap year): \( 100 \) years = \( 25 \) leap

Q: A train 200 m long is running with a speed of 54 kmph. In what will it pass a car whose speed at 36 kmph in the direction opposite to that in which the train is going
Options:
A)8 sec
B)10 sec
C)12 sec
D)14 sec
E)15 sec
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The train is 200 meters long.
- The train's speed is 54 km/h.
- The car's speed is 36 km/h.
- The car is moving in the opposite direction to the train.
- We need to calculate the time it takes for the train to completely pass the car.
- Assumptions:
  - Both the train and the car are moving at constant speeds.
  - The relative speed is the sum of the train's and car's speeds because they are moving in opposite directions.
  - The time taken to pass is calculated as the distance (length of the train) divided by the relative speed.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Convert speeds to meters per second and calculate time directly.**
- Convert the train's and car's speeds from km/h to m/s.
- Add the relative speeds since they are moving in opposite directions.
- Use the formula: \( \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Relative Speed}} \).

**Method 2: Work with speeds in km/h and convert the time to seconds at the end.**
- Add the relative speeds in km/h.
- Use the formula: \( \text{Time (in hours)} = \frac{\text{Distance (in km)}}{\text{Relative Speed (in km/h)}} \).
- Convert the time from hours to seconds.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Convert speeds to meters per second and calculate time directly.**
1. Convert the train's speed from km/h to m/s:
   \[
   54 \, \text{km/h} = \frac{54 \times 1000}{3600} = 15 \, \text{m/s}.
   \]
2. Convert the car's speed from km/h to m/s:
   \[
   36 \, \text{km/h} = \frac{36 \times 1000}{3600} = 10 \, \text{m/s}.
   \]
3. Calculate the relative speed (since they are moving in opposite directions):
   \[
   \text{Relative Speed} = 15 + 10 = 25 \, \text{m/s}.
   \]
4. Use the formula \( \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Relative Speed}} \):
   \[
   \text{Time} = \frac{200}{25} = 8 \, \text{seconds}.
   \]

#### **Method 2: Work with speeds in km/h and convert the time to seconds at the end.**
1. Add the relative speeds in km/h:
   \[
   \text{Relative Speed} = 54 + 36 = 90 \, \text{km/h}.
   \]
2. Convert the train's length from meters to kilometers:
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \frac{200}{1000} = 0.2 \, \text{km}.
   \]
3. Use the formula \( \text{Time (in hours)} = \frac{\text{Distance (in km)}}{\text{Relative Speed (in km/h)}} \):
   \[
   \text{Time (in hours)} = \frac{0.2}{90} = 0.002222 \, \text{hours}.
   \]
4. Convert the time from hours to seconds:
   \[
   \text{Time (in seconds)} = 0.002222 \times 3600 = 8 \, \text{seconds}.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward because it works directly with meters per second, which is the standard unit for speed in physics. It avoids unnecessary conversions and is less prone to rounding errors.
- **Method 2** involves more conversions (meters to kilometers, hours to seconds), which increases the chance of mistakes and makes the process longer.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and directness.
- Using Method 1:
  \[
  \text{Time} = \frac{200}{25} = 8 \, \text{seconds}.
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- The relative speed of 25 m/s is reasonable for a train and car moving in opposite directions.
- The time of 8 seconds is reasonable for a train of 200 meters passing a car at this relative speed.
- Both

❌ INCORRECT OR INVALID
Q: A, B and C can do a piece of work in 9 days, 14 days and 28 days respectively. How long will they taken, if all the three work together?
Options:
A)4.12 days
B)4.52 days
C)3.52 days
D)2.52 days
E)4.92 days
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- A can complete the work in 9 days, so A's work rate is \( \frac{1}{9} \) of the work per day.
- B can complete the work in 14 days, so B's work rate is \( \frac{1}{14} \) of the work per day.
- C can complete the work in 28 days, so C's work rate is \( \frac{1}{28} \) of the work per day.
- When A, B, and C work together, their combined work rate is the sum of their individual work rates.
- The total time taken to complete the work is the reciprocal of their combined work rate.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Direct addition of work rates.
- Add the work rates of A, B, and C: \( \frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{14} + \frac{1}{28} \).
- Find the least common denominator (LCD) to simplify the addition.
- Take the reciprocal of the combined work rate to find the total time.

#### Method 2: Convert work rates to a common unit of work.
- Assume the total work is 1 unit.
- Calculate how much work A, B, and C can do together in one day.
- Use the formula \( \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Total Work}}{\text{Combined Work Rate}} \) to find the total time.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Direct addition of work rates.
1. Write the work rates: \( \frac{1}{9}, \frac{1}{14}, \frac{1}{28} \).
2. Find the least common denominator (LCD) of 9, 14, and 28:
   - Prime factorization: \( 9 = 3^2 \), \( 14 = 2 \times 7 \), \( 28 = 2^2 \times 7 \).
   - LCD = \( 2^2 \times 3^2 \times 7 = 252 \).
3. Rewrite the fractions with the LCD as the denominator:
   - \( \frac{1}{9} = \frac{28}{252} \),
   - \( \frac{1}{14} = \frac{18}{252} \),
   - \( \frac{1}{28} = \frac{9}{252} \).
4. Add the fractions:
   \[
   \frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{14} + \frac{1}{28} = \frac{28}{252} + \frac{18}{252} + \frac{9}{252} = \frac{55}{252}.
   \]
5. The combined work rate is \( \frac{55}{252} \) of the work per day.
6. The total time to complete the work is the reciprocal of the combined work rate:
   \[
   \text{Time} = \frac{1}{\frac{55}{252}} = \frac{252}{55} \approx 4.58 \, \text{days}.
   \]

#### Method 2: Convert work rates to a common unit of work.
1. Assume the total work is 1 unit.
2. Calculate how much work A, B, and C can do together in one day:
   \[
   \text{Combined Work Rate} = \frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{14} + \frac{1}{28}.
   \]
3. Use the same LCD and addition as in Method 1:
   \[
   \frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{14} + \frac{1}{28} = \frac{55}{252}.
   \]
4. The total time to complete the work is:
   \[
   \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Total Work}}{\text{Combined Work Rate}} = \frac{1}{\frac{55}{252}} = \frac{252}{55} \approx 4.58 \, \text{days}.
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods involve the same calculations and yield the same result.
- Method 1 is more direct and intuitive for adding work rates.
- Method 2 is useful for understanding the relationship between total work and work rates but involves the same steps.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Method 1 is chosen for its simplicity.
- The total time is \( \frac{252}{55} \approx 4.58 \, \text{days} \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.
Extracted Option:

  \[
  \text{Number of permutations} = \frac{n!}{p_1! \cdot p_2! \cdot \dots \cdot p_k!}

❌ INCORRECT OR INVALID
Q: A man has Rs. 288 in the denominations of one-rupee notes, five-rupee notes and ten-rupee notes. The number of notes of each denomination is equal. What is the total number of notes that he has ?
Options:
A)45
B)60
C)75
D)18
E)95
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The man has Rs. 288 in total.
- He has notes in three denominations: Rs. 1, Rs. 5, and Rs. 10.
- The number of notes of each denomination is equal.
- Let the number of notes of each denomination be \( x \).
- The total value of the notes is calculated as:
  \[
  \text{Total value} = (1 \times x) + (5 \times x) + (10 \times x) = 288
  \]
- We assume all notes are valid and there are no fractional notes.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Solve using algebra.**
- Use the equation for the total value of the notes to find \( x \), the number of notes of each denomination.
- Once \( x \) is found, calculate the total number of notes as \( 3x \) (since there are three denominations).

**Method 2: Solve using trial and error.**
- Assume different values for \( x \) and calculate the total value of the notes using the formula:
  \[
  \text{Total value} = (1 \times x) + (5 \times x) + (10 \times x)
  \]
- Check which value of \( x \) satisfies the total value of Rs. 288.
- Once \( x \) is found, calculate the total number of notes as \( 3x \).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail.

#### **Method 1: Solve using algebra.**
1. Write the equation for the total value:
   \[
   (1 \times x) + (5 \times x) + (10 \times x) = 288
   \]
2. Simplify the equation:
   \[
   x + 5x + 10x = 288
   \]
   \[
   16x = 288
   \]
3. Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   x = \frac{288}{16} = 18
   \]
4. Calculate the total number of notes:
   \[
   \text{Total notes} = 3x = 3 \times 18 = 54
   \]

#### **Method 2: Solve using trial and error.**

4. Calculate the total number of notes:

   \]

- **Method 2 (trial and error)** is less efficient but can be useful for verifying the solution or if the equation is not easily solvable.
- Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is preferred for clarity and reliability.

B)90
C)100
D)105
E)140
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The apartment building has **490 units** in total.
- **4 out of every 7 units** are currently rented.
- The building consists only of **two-bedroom** and **one-bedroom** units.
- Of the rented apartments:
  - There is a **6:1 ratio** of two-bedroom units to one-bedroom units.
  - **1/4 of the one-bedroom units** are rented.
- We are tasked with finding how many **two-bedroom units are not rented**.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Direct calculation using ratios and proportions.
1. Calculate the total number of rented units.
2. Use the 6:1 ratio to determine the number of rented two-bedroom and one-bedroom units.
3. Calculate the total number of one-bedroom units in the building using the fact that 1/4 of them are rented.
4. Subtract the rented two-bedroom units from the total two-bedroom units to find the number of unrented two-bedroom units.

#### Method 2: Solve using algebra.
1. Let the total number of one-bedroom units be \( x \) and the total number of two-bedroom units be \( 490 - x \).
2. Use the fact that 1/4 of the one-bedroom units are rented and the 6:1 ratio of rented two-bedroom to one-bedroom units to set up equations.
3. Solve for \( x \) and calculate the number of unrented two-bedroom units.

1. **Calculate the total number of rented units:**
   - \( \frac{4}{7} \) of the 490 units are rented:
     \[
     \text{Rented units} = \frac{4}{7} \times 490 = 280.
     \]

     \]

     \[
     \text{Total one-bedroom units} = 4 \times \text{Rented one-bedroom units} = 4 \times 40 = 160.
     \]

     \[
     \text{Total two-bedroom units} = 490 - 160 = 330.

   - Unrented two-bedroom units = Total two-bedroom units - Rented two-bedroom units:
     \[
     \text{Unrented two-bedroom units} = 330 - 240 = 90.

#### Method 2: Algebraic approach.
1. **Define variables:**
   - Let \( x \) = total number of one-bedroom units.
   - Then \( 490 - x \) = total number of two-bedroom units.

     \[
     \text{Rented one-bedroom units} = \frac{1}{4}x.
     \]
   - The 6:1 ratio implies:
     \[
     \text{Rented two-bedroom units} = 6 \times \text{Rented one-bedroom units} = 6 \times \frac{1}{4}x = \frac{3}{2}x.
     \]
   - Total rented units = 280:
     \[
     \frac{1}{4}x + \frac{3}{2}x = 280.
     \]

3. **Solve for \( x \):**

     \[
     \frac{1}{4}x + \frac{3}{2}x = \frac{1}{4}x + \frac{6}{4}x = \frac{7}{4}x.
     \]
   - Solve for \( x \):
     \[
     \frac{7}{4}x = 280 \implies x = 280 \times \frac{4}{7} = 160.
     \]

- We need to calculate the **minimum number of cubes** required to build the box.
- Assumptions:
  - The box is completely filled with no gaps.
  - The cubes are perfectly packed without any leftover space.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### **Method 1: Calculate the total volume of the box and divide by the volume of one cube.**
- The total volume of the box is given by multiplying its dimensions: \( \text{Volume} = \text{length} \times \text{width} \times \text{height} \).
- The number of cubes required is the total volume of the box divided by the volume of one cube.

#### **Method 2: Break the box into smaller sections and calculate the number of cubes for each section.**
- Divide the box into smaller layers or sections based on one dimension (e.g., height).
- Calculate the number of cubes required for each layer and sum them up.

   \[

     \[
     \text{Cubes per layer} = \frac{\text{Base area}}{\text{Base area of one cube}} = \frac{130}{5} = 26.
     \]
4. Multiply by the number of layers (equal to the height of the box in cm):
   \[
   \text{Total cubes} = \text{Cubes per layer} \times \text{Number of layers} = 26 \times 5 = 130.
   \]
5. Check for potential pitfalls:
   - Ensure the base area is divisible by the cube base area. Since 130 is divisible by 5, the calculation is valid.

---

- **Method 1** is simpler and more direct, as it involves calculating the total volume and dividing by the cube volume.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and involves breaking the problem into smaller parts, which can be useful for visualizing the solution but is less efficient.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and reliable for this problem.

   \]

   - Storage cost: \( x \times 0.20 = 0.2x \).

8. **September**: At the beginning of September, \( x \) rakes are stored. \( x/2 \) rakes are shipped, leaving \( x - x/2 = 0.5x \) rakes stored.

9. **October**: At the beginning of October, \( 0.5x \) rakes are stored. \( x/2 \) rakes are shipped, leaving \( 0.5x - x/2 = 0 \) rakes stored.

    \[

C)1 hour and 40 minutes.
D)2 hours and 10 minutes
E)1 hour and 20 minutes

### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Avery can build a brick wall in **2.5 hours**, so his work rate is \( \frac{1}{2.5} = 0.4 \) walls per hour.
- Tom can build a brick wall in **4 hours**, so his work rate is \( \frac{1}{4} = 0.25 \) walls per hour.
- Avery and Tom work together for **1 hour**, after which Avery leaves.
- We need to calculate how much of the wall is completed after 1 hour of joint work and determine how much time it will take Tom to finish the remaining portion of the wall on his own.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.

2. Determine how much of the wall is completed in 1 hour.

#### Method 2: Proportional reasoning.

   \[
   \text{Fraction remaining} = 1 - 0.65 = 0.35.

   Convert 1.4 hours to minutes:
   \[
   1.4 \text{ hours} = 1 \text{ hour and } 0.4 \times 60 = 24 \text{ minutes}.
   \]
   Total time = **1 hour and 24 minutes**.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods yield the same result: **1 hour and 24 minutes**.
- Method 1 is more straightforward and involves fewer steps, making it easier to follow.
- Method 2 uses proportional reasoning, which is helpful for understanding the problem conceptually but may introduce unnecessary complexity.

- \(21!\) represents the factorial of 21, i.e., \(21 \times 20 \times \dots \times 1\).  
- We assume standard factorial rules and arithmetic operations.  
- The options are:  
  A) 49  
  B) 36  
  C) 1  
  D) 16  
  E) 25  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Factorize \(22!\) and \(21!\) to simplify the expression.  
- Recognize that \(22! = 22 \times 21!\), which allows us to rewrite the numerator and denominator in terms of \(21!\).  
- Simplify the expression step by step to make calculations manageable.  

#### Method 2: Approximate the factorials.  
- Use the fact that factorials grow very quickly, so \(22!\) is much larger than \(21!\).  
- Approximate the terms in the numerator and denominator to simplify the calculation.

   - Denominator: \(22! - 21! \approx 22 \times 21! - 21! = 21 \times 21!\).  
2. Simplify the fraction:  
   \[
   \frac{22! + 21!}{22! - 21!} \approx \frac{23 \times 21!}{21 \times 21!}.
   \]
3. Cancel \(21!\) from the numerator and denominator:  
   \[
   \frac{23 \times 21!}{21 \times 21!} = \frac{23}{21}.
   \]
4. Simplify \(\frac{23}{21}\):  
   \[
   \frac{23}{21} \approx 1.095.
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods yield the same result: \(\frac{23}{21} \approx 1.095\).  
- Method 1 is more rigorous and involves clear algebraic manipulation.  
- Method 2 relies on approximations but is faster and still accurate.

Q: A train overtakes 2 girls who are walking inthe opposite direction in which the train is going at the rate of 3 km/hour & 6km/hour and passes them completely in 36sec & 30 sec respectively. Find the length of the train is?
Options:
A)130 m
B)140 m
C)150 m
D)170 m
E)190 m
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The train overtakes two girls walking in the opposite direction of the train.
- The speed of the first girl is **3 km/h**, and the train passes her completely in **36 seconds**.
- The speed of the second girl is **6 km/h**, and the train passes her completely in **30 seconds**.
- The length of the train is the same in both cases.
- The train's speed is constant.
- We assume the speeds are relative to the ground, and the train's speed is greater than the girls' speeds.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Use the relative speed formula.
- The relative speed between the train and each girl is the train's speed minus the girl's speed (since they are moving in opposite directions).
- The length of the train can be calculated using the formula:  
  \[
  \text{Length of train} = \text{Relative speed} \times \text{Time taken to pass}
  \]
- Convert speeds from km/h to m/s for consistency.

#### Method 2: Set up equations for both cases and solve for the train's length.

### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.

- \(4! = 4 \cdot 3 \cdot 2 \cdot 1 = 24\).  
- \(3! = 3 \cdot 2 \cdot 1 = 6\).  
- \(2! = 2 \cdot 1 = 2\).  
- The "tenth digit" refers to the digit in the tens place of the final result.  
- We assume all calculations are performed correctly and no rounding is involved.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct computation of the expression.**

3. Simplify step by step to find the final result.  
4. Extract the tenth digit from the result.

**Method 2: Factorization and simplification.**  
1. Factor \(4!\) from the numerator: \(4! \cdot (3! + 2!)\).  
2. Compute \(3! + 2!\), then multiply by \(4!\).  
3. Divide the result by 3.  
4. Extract the tenth digit from the result.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail.

#### **Method 1: Direct computation**
1. Compute factorials:  
   - \(4! = 24\), \(3! = 6\), \(2! = 2\).

   \[
   (4! \cdot 3! + 4! \cdot 2!) / 3 = (24 \cdot 6 + 24 \cdot 2) / 3.
   \]

3. Simplify the numerator:  
   - \(24 \cdot 6 = 144\), \(24 \cdot 2 = 48\).  
   - \(144 + 48 = 192\).  

4. Divide by 3:  
   \[
   192 / 3 = 64.
   \]

5. Extract the tenth digit:  
   - The result is \(64\), and the digit in the tens place is **6**.

---

#### **Method 2: Factorization and simplification**

   \[
   (4! \cdot 3! + 4! \cdot 2!) = 4! \cdot (3! + 2!).
   \]

2. Compute \(3! + 2!\):  
   - \(3! = 6\), \(2! = 2\).

3. Multiply by \(4!\):  
   \[
   4! \cdot (3! + 2!) = 24 \cdot 8 = 192.
   \]

4. Divide by 3:  
   \[
   192 / 3 = 64.
   \]

5. Extract the tenth digit:  
   - The result is \(64\), and the digit in the tens place is **6**.

---

- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 2** is preferred for its clarity and efficiency.

---

1. Factorize: \(4! \cdot (3! + 2!) = 24 \cdot 8 = 192\).  
2. Divide: \(192 / 3 = 64\).  
3. The tenth digit of \(64\) is **6**.

- Let the number of hens = \( h \) and the number of sheep = \( s \).
- From the total heads: \( h + s = 38 \).
- From the total legs: \( 2h + 4s = 100 \).

**Method 2: Trial and error**

   \]
   \[
   2h = 52

   \[
   h = 26
   \]

   \[
   s = 38 - 26 = 12
   \]
3. Verify the solution:
   - Total heads: \( h + s = 26 + 12 = 38 \) (correct).
   - Total legs: \( 2h + 4s = 2(26) + 4(12) = 52 + 48 = 100 \) (correct).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (System of equations)** is more systematic and reliable, especially for problems involving simultaneous equations. It avoids guesswork and ensures accuracy.
- **Method 2 (Trial and error)** is faster for small numbers but can become cumbersome for larger or more complex problems.

B)24 years
C)25 years
D)30 years
E)None
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and assumptions.
- The cricket team has 11 members.
- The captain is 26 years old.
- The wicket keeper is 3 years older than the captain, so the wicket keeper is 29 years old.
- If the ages of the captain and wicket keeper are excluded, the average age of the remaining 9 players is one year less than the average age of the whole team.
- We need to find the average age of the whole team.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use algebra to represent the average age of the team and solve step by step.
- Let the average age of the whole team be \( x \).
- The total age of the team is \( 11x \).
- The total age of the captain and wicket keeper is \( 26 + 29 = 55 \).
- The total age of the remaining 9 players is \( 11x - 55 \).
- The average age of the remaining 9 players is \( \frac{11x - 55}{9} \), which is given to be \( x - 1 \).

- Calculate the total age of the team for each option and check if the condition about the average age of the remaining 9 players holds true.

   \[
   \frac{11x - 55}{9} = x - 1
   \]

   \[
   11x - 55 = 9(x - 1)
   \]
8. Expand and simplify:
   \[
   11x - 55 = 9x - 9
   \]
   \[
   11x - 9x = 55 - 9
   \]
   \[
   2x = 46
   \]
   \[
   x = 23
   \]

     - Total age of the team: \( 11 \times 23 = 253 \).
     - Total age of the captain and wicket keeper: \( 26 + 29 = 55 \).
     - Total age of the remaining 9 players: \( 253 - 55 = 198 \).
     - Average age of the remaining 9 players: \( \frac{198}{9} = 22 \), which is \( x - 1 = 23 - 1 = 22 \). This satisfies the condition.

     - Total age of the team: \( 11 \times 24 = 264 \).
     - Total age of the captain and wicket keeper: \( 26 + 29 = 55 \).
     - Total age of the remaining 9 players: \( 264 - 55 = 209 \).
     - Average age of the remaining 9 players: \( \frac{209}{9} \approx 23.22 \), which does not satisfy the condition.

     - Total age of the team: \( 11 \times 25 = 275 \).
     - Total age of the captain and wicket keeper: \( 26 + 29 = 55 \).
     - Total age of the remaining 9 players: \( 275 - 55 = 220 \).
     - Average age of the remaining 9 players: \( \frac{220}{9} \approx 24.44 \), which does not satisfy the condition.
   - Option D: \( x = 30 \)
     - Total age of the team: \( 11 \times 30 = 330 \).

Q: A work could be completed in 100days by x workers. However, due to the absence of 10workers, it was completed in 110days. The original no.of workers was:
Options:
A)90
B)106
C)110
D)120
E)130
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- A work can be completed in **100 days** by **x workers**.
- Due to the absence of **10 workers**, the work was completed in **110 days**.
- Work is assumed to be evenly distributed among workers, and the total amount of work remains constant.
- The relationship between workers, time, and work is inversely proportional: fewer workers require more time to complete the same work.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Use the concept of "work done" to set up an equation.
- Total work = Number of workers × Time.
- Let the total work be \( W \). For \( x \) workers, \( W = 100x \).
- With \( x - 10 \) workers, the same work \( W \) is completed in 110 days: \( W = 110(x - 10) \).
- Solve for \( x \) by equating the two expressions for \( W \).

#### Method 2: Use the concept of "work rate" to set up an equation.
- Work rate = \( \frac{\text{Total work}}{\text{Time}} \).
- The work rate of \( x \) workers is \( \frac{W}{100} \), and the work rate of \( x - 10 \) workers is \( \frac{W}{110} \).
- The work rate is proportional to the number of workers, so \( \frac{x}{100} = \frac{x - 10}{110} \).
- Solve for \( x \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.
#### Method 1: Using total work.
1. Total work for \( x \) workers: \( W = 100x \).
2. Total work for \( x - 10 \) workers: \( W = 110(x - 10) \).
3. Equate the two expressions for \( W \):
   \[
   100x = 110(x - 10)
   \]
4. Expand and simplify:
   \[
   100x = 110x - 1100
   \]
   \[
   100x - 110x = -1100
   \]
   \[
   -10x = -1100
   \]
   \[
   x = 110
   \]
5. Check: If \( x = 110 \), then the original number of workers is 110. With 10 workers absent, \( x - 10 = 100 \), and the work is completed in 110 days. This satisfies the problem conditions.

#### Method 2: Using work rate.
1. Work rate of \( x \) workers: \( \frac{x}{100} \).
2. Work rate of \( x - 10 \) workers: \( \frac{x - 10}{110} \).
3. Equate the two work rates:
   \[
   \frac{x}{100} = \frac{x - 10}{110}
   \]
4. Cross-multiply:
   \[
   110x = 100(x - 10)
   \]
5. Expand and simplify:
   \[
   110x = 100x - 1000
   \]
   \[
   110x - 100x = -1000
   \]
   \[
   10x = 1000
   \]
   \[
   x = 110
   \]
6. Check: If \( x = 110 \), then the original number of workers is 110. With 10 workers absent, \( x - 10 = 100 \), and the work is completed in 110 days. This satisfies the problem conditions.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** (using total work) directly relates the total work done by the workers and is straightforward to set up and solve.
- **Method 2** (using work rate) is slightly more abstract but also leads to the correct result. It is useful for problems involving proportional relationships.
- Both methods are reliable and efficient, but Method 1 is slightly more intuitive for this problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- Method 1 is chosen for its simplicity and clarity.
- Solution: \( x = 110 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Original number of workers: \( x = 110 \).
- With 10 workers absent, \( x - 10 = 100 \).
- Work completed in 100 days by 110 workers: \( 100 \times 110 = 11000 \)

- Assumptions:
  - \( X \) is a positive integer.
  - Divisibility means \( X \) can be divided by the given number without leaving a remainder.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### Method 1: Analyze divisibility using the least common multiple (LCM).
- Since \( X \) is divisible by 3, 6, and 12, it must be divisible by their least common multiple (LCM). 
- Check whether the LCM of 3, 6, and 12 implies divisibility by each of the options (12, 35, and 46).

#### Method 2: Test divisibility directly for each option.
- For each option (12, 35, and 46), check whether \( X \) being divisible by 3, 6, and 12 guarantees divisibility by that option:
  - \( I. \) 12: Check if \( X \) is divisible by 12.
  - \( II. \) 35: Check if \( X \) is divisible by 35.
  - \( III. \) 46: Check if \( X \) is divisible by 46.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail.

#### Method 1: Analyze divisibility using the LCM.
1. Find the LCM of 3, 6, and 12:
   - The prime factorization of 3 is \( 3 \).
   - The prime factorization of 6 is \( 2 \times 3 \).
   - The prime factorization of 12 is \( 2^2 \times 3 \).
   - The LCM is \( 2^2 \times 3 = 12 \).
2. Since \( X \) is divisible by 3, 6, and 12, \( X \) must be divisible by their LCM, which is 12.
3. Check each option:
   - \( I. \) 12: \( X \) is divisible by 12 because it is the LCM.
   - \( II. \) 35: The prime factorization of 35 is \( 5 \times 7 \). None of these factors are present in 3, 6, or 12, so \( X \) is not guaranteed to be divisible by 35.
   - \( III. \) 46: The prime factorization of 46 is \( 2 \times 23 \). The factor 23 is not present in 3, 6, or 12, so \( X \) is not guaranteed to be divisible by 46.

#### Method 2: Test divisibility directly for each option.
1. \( I. \) 12:
   - Since \( X \) is divisible by 12, it is guaranteed to be divisible by 12.
2. \( II. \) 35:
   - For \( X \) to be divisible by 35, it must be divisible by both 5 and 7.
   - \( X \) is divisible by 3, 6, and 12, but none of these numbers include the factors 5 or 7. Therefore, \( X \) is not guaranteed to be divisible by 35.
3. \( III. \) 46:
   - For \( X \) to be divisible by 46, it must be divisible by both 2 and 23.
   - \( X \) is divisible by 3, 6, and 12, but none of these numbers include the factor 23. Therefore, \( X \) is not guaranteed to be divisible by 46.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more systematic because it uses the LCM to determine the guaranteed divisors of \( X \), making it more efficient and reliable.
- **Method 2** involves testing each option individually, which is less efficient but still valid.

  - \( I. \) \( X \) is divisible by 12.

Q: From the starting point in a boat race, one competitor started to sail north at a speed of 1.5 Km/h, the other competitor started to sail west at a speed of 1.2 Km/h. What is the distance in Km between the two competitors after 5 hours?
Options:
A)9.6
B)12.
C)12.5.
D)14.
E)15.4.
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Competitor 1 sails north at a speed of 1.5 km/h.
- Competitor 2 sails west at a speed of 1.2 km/h.
- Both competitors sail for 5 hours.
- The distance between the two competitors after 5 hours forms the hypotenuse of a right triangle, where the northward and westward distances are the two legs.
- We assume constant speeds and straight-line paths.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Use the Pythagorean theorem.**
- Calculate the northward distance traveled by Competitor 1: \( \text{distance} = \text{speed} \times \text{time} \).
- Calculate the westward distance traveled by Competitor 2: \( \text{distance} = \text{speed} \times \text{time} \).
- Use the Pythagorean theorem to find the hypotenuse: \( c = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2} \), where \( a \) and \( b \) are the distances traveled north and west.

**Method 2: Use vector magnitude.**
- Represent the competitors' movements as vectors: \( \vec{v_1} = (0, 1.5 \times 5) \) for northward movement and \( \vec{v_2} = (1.2 \times 5, 0) \) for westward movement.
- The distance between the competitors is the magnitude of the resultant vector: \( |\vec{v}| = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2} \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Use the Pythagorean theorem.**
1. Calculate the northward distance traveled by Competitor 1:
   \[
   \text{Northward distance} = 1.5 \, \text{km/h} \times 5 \, \text{hours} = 7.5 \, \text{km}.
   \]
2. Calculate the westward distance traveled by Competitor 2:
   \[
   \text{Westward distance} = 1.2 \, \text{km/h} \times 5 \, \text{hours} = 6.0 \, \text{km}.
   \]
3. Use the Pythagorean theorem to find the distance between the competitors:
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \sqrt{(7.5)^2 + (6.0)^2}.
   \]
4. Calculate the squares:
   \[
   (7.5)^2 = 56.25, \quad (6.0)^2 = 36.0.
   \]
5. Add the squares:
   \[
   56.25 + 36.0 = 92.25.
   \]
6. Take the square root:
   \[
   \sqrt{92.25} = 9.6 \, \text{km}.
   \]

#### **Method 2: Use vector magnitude.**
1. Represent the movements as vectors:
   - Competitor 1: \( \vec{v_1} = (0, 7.5) \).
   - Competitor 2: \( \vec{v_2} = (6.0, 0) \).
2. Find the resultant vector:
   \[
   \vec{v} = \vec{v_2} - \vec{v_1} = (6.0 - 0, 0 - 7.5) = (6.0, -7.5).
   \]
3. Calculate the magnitude of the resultant vector:
   \[
   |\vec{v}| = \sqrt{(6.0)^2 + (-7.5)^2}.
   \]
4. Calculate the squares:
   \[
   (6.0)^2 = 36.0, \quad (-7.5)^2 = 56.25.
   \]
5. Add the squares:
   \[
   36.0 + 56.25 = 92.25.
   \]
6. Take the square root:
   \[
   \sqrt{92.25} = 9.6 \, \text{km}.
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods yield the same result.
- **Method 1** is simpler and more intuitive for this problem, as it directly uses the Pythagorean theorem without requiring vector representation.

Q: In a certain store, the profit is 320% of the cost. If the cost increases by 35% but the selling price
remains constant, approximately what percentage of the selling price is the profit
Options:
A)68%
B)80%
C)90%
D)100%
E)None of above
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The profit is **320% of the cost**. This means the profit is 3.2 times the cost.
- The selling price is the sum of the cost and the profit:  
  \[
  \text{Selling Price} = \text{Cost} + \text{Profit} = \text{Cost} + 3.2 \times \text{Cost} = 4.2 \times \text{Cost}.
  \]
- The cost increases by **35%**, so the new cost becomes:  
  \[
  \text{New Cost} = 1.35 \times \text{Original Cost}.
  \]
- The selling price remains constant.
- We are tasked with finding the new profit as a percentage of the selling price.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use algebra to express the new profit and calculate its percentage of the selling price.
1. Calculate the new profit as the difference between the selling price and the new cost.
2. Express the new profit as a percentage of the selling price.

#### Method 2: Assume a specific value for the original cost (e.g., 100) to simplify calculations.
1. Calculate the original selling price and profit based on the assumed cost.
2. Calculate the new cost and the new profit.
3. Express the new profit as a percentage of the selling price.

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
---

#### **Method 1: Algebraic Approach**
1. Let the original cost be \( C \).  
   - Original selling price:  
     \[
     \text{Selling Price} = 4.2 \times C.
     \]
   - New cost:  
     \[
     \text{New Cost} = 1.35 \times C.
     \]
   - New profit:  
     \[
     \text{New Profit} = \text{Selling Price} - \text{New Cost} = 4.2 \times C - 1.35 \times C = 2.85 \times C.
     \]
2. Express the new profit as a percentage of the selling price:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage of Selling Price} = \frac{\text{New Profit}}{\text{Selling Price}} \times 100 = \frac{2.85 \times C}{4.2 \times C} \times 100.
   \]
3. Simplify:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage of Selling Price} = \frac{2.85}{4.2} \times 100 = 0.67857 \times 100 \approx 68\%.
   \]

---

#### **Method 2: Assume a Specific Value for the Original Cost**
1. Assume the original cost is \( C = 100 \).  
   - Original selling price:  
     \[
     \text{Selling Price} = 4.2 \times 100 = 420.
     \]
   - Original profit:  
     \[
     \text{Profit} = 3.2 \times 100 = 320.
     \]
2. Calculate the new cost:  
   \[
   \text{New Cost} = 1.35 \times 100 = 135.
   \]
3. Calculate the new profit:  
   \[
   \text{New Profit} = \text{Selling Price} - \text{New Cost} = 420 - 135 = 285.
   \]
4. Express the new profit as a percentage of the selling price:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage of Selling Price} = \frac{\text{New Profit}}{\text{Selling Price}} \times 100 = \frac{285}{420} \times 100.
   \]
5. Simplify:  
   \[
   \text{Percentage of Selling Price} = 0.67857 \times 100 \approx 68\%.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more general and does not rely on specific assumptions about the cost. It is algebraic and works for any value of the cost.
- **Method 2** is simpler and faster but relies on assuming a specific value for the cost, which may not always be appropriate.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- **Method 1** is better because it is more general and does not depend on specific assumptions.  
- Using Method 1, the new profit as a percentage of the selling price is approximately **68%**.
