Q: A shipment of 240 smartphones contains 84 that are defective. If a customer buys two smartphones at random from the shipment, what is the approximate probability that both phones are defective?
Options:
A)1/250
B)9/74
C)1/11
D)1/9
E)1/3
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Total smartphones in the shipment: 240.
- Number of defective smartphones: 84.
- A customer buys two smartphones at random.
- We are tasked with finding the approximate probability that **both phones are defective**.
- Assumptions:
  - The selection is random.
  - The probability is calculated without replacement (i.e., once a phone is selected, it is not returned to the shipment).

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### Method 1: Use the formula for probability without replacement.
- The probability of selecting the first defective phone is \( \frac{84}{240} \).
- After selecting the first defective phone, there are \( 83 \) defective phones left out of \( 239 \) total phones.
- Multiply these probabilities to find the probability of selecting two defective phones.

#### Method 2: Approximate the probability using proportions.
- The proportion of defective phones in the shipment is \( \frac{84}{240} \).
- The probability of selecting two defective phones can be approximated as \( \left(\frac{84}{240}\right)^2 \), assuming the shipment is large enough that the change in total phones after the first selection is negligible.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### Method 1: Probability without replacement
1. Probability of selecting the first defective phone:
   \[
   P(\text{first defective}) = \frac{84}{240}.
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   P(\text{first defective}) = \frac{7}{20}.
   \]

2. Probability of selecting the second defective phone (after the first defective phone is removed):
   \[
   P(\text{second defective | first defective}) = \frac{83}{239}.
   \]

3. Multiply the probabilities:
   \[
   P(\text{both defective}) = \frac{7}{20} \times \frac{83}{239}.
   \]

4. Perform the multiplication:
   \[
   P(\text{both defective}) = \frac{7 \times 83}{20 \times 239} = \frac{581}{4780}.
   \]

5. Approximate the result:
   \[
   \frac{581}{4780} \approx 0.1215.
   \]

---

#### Method 2: Approximation using proportions
1. Proportion of defective phones:
   \[
   \frac{84}{240} = 0.35.
   \]

2. Approximate the probability of selecting two defective phones:
   \[
   P(\text{both defective}) \approx 0.35 \times 0.35 = 0.1225.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more precise because it accounts for the reduction in total phones after the first selection (i.e., no replacement). It is slightly more complex but provides an exact result.
- **Method 2** is faster and simpler but introduces a small error by assuming replacement or negligible change in proportions.

Since the shipment size is not extremely large, **Method 1** is preferred for accuracy.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
\[
P(\text{both defective}) = \frac{7}{20} \times \frac{83}{239} = \frac{581}{4780}.
\]
Approximating:
\[
\frac{581}{4780} \approx 0.1215.
\]

Convert to a fraction:
\[
0.1215 \approx \frac{1}{11}.
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- The probability is reasonable given that 35% of the phones are defective.
- Both methods yield similar results (\( \approx 0.1215 \) and \( \approx 0.1225 \)).
- The closest option is \( \frac{1}{11} \), which matches the calculated probability.

Options:
A)1059
B)1028
C)1052
D)1028
E)1012
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Principal (P) = Rs. 800.
- Amount after 3 years = Rs. 956.
- Time (T) = 3 years.
- Simple Interest (S.I.) formula: \( S.I. = \frac{P \cdot R \cdot T}{100} \), where \( R \) is the rate of interest.
- The rate of interest \( R \) is initially unknown and needs to be calculated.
- If the rate of interest is increased by 4%, we need to calculate the new amount after 3 years.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: 
1. Calculate the original rate of interest \( R \) using the given data.
2. Increase \( R \) by 4% to get the new rate.
3. Use the new rate to calculate the new amount after 3 years.

#### Method 2:
1. Calculate the original simple interest (S.I.) using the given data.
2. Calculate the new simple interest by increasing the rate by 4%.
3. Add the new simple interest to the principal to find the new amount.

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail.
#### Method 1:
1. **Calculate the original rate of interest \( R \):**
   - From the given data, \( S.I. = \text{Amount} - \text{Principal} = 956 - 800 = 156 \).
   - Using the formula \( S.I. = \frac{P \cdot R \cdot T}{100} \):
     \[
     156 = \frac{800 \cdot R \cdot 3}{100}
     \]
     Simplify:
     \[
     156 = \frac{2400 \cdot R}{100}
     \]
     \[
     156 = 24R
     \]
     \[
     R = \frac{156}{24} = 6\% \text{ per year.}
     \]

2. **Increase the rate by 4%:**
   - New rate \( R_{\text{new}} = 6\% + 4\% = 10\% \).

3. **Calculate the new amount:**
   - Using the formula \( S.I. = \frac{P \cdot R_{\text{new}} \cdot T}{100} \):
     \[
     S.I. = \frac{800 \cdot 10 \cdot 3}{100} = \frac{24000}{100} = 240
     \]
   - New amount = Principal + New S.I. = 800 + 240 = 1040.

#### Method 2:
1. **Calculate the original simple interest (S.I.):**
   - From the given data, \( S.I. = 956 - 800 = 156 \).

2. **Calculate the new simple interest:**
   - Original rate \( R = 6\% \), so new rate \( R_{\text{new}} = 6\% + 4\% = 10\%.
   - New S.I. = \( \frac{P \cdot R_{\text{new}} \cdot T}{100} \):
     \[
     S.I. = \frac{800 \cdot 10 \cdot 3}{100} = 240
     \]

3. **Calculate the new amount:**
   - New amount = Principal + New S.I. = 800 + 240 = 1040.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods involve similar steps and yield the same result.
- Method 1 is slightly more structured as it explicitly calculates the rate first, making it easier to follow.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Using Method 1:
  - Original rate \( R = 6\% \).
  - New rate \( R_{\text{new}} = 10\% \).
  - New S.I. = 240.
  - New amount = 800 + 240 = 1040.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution.
- Recalculate the new S.I.:
  \[
  S.I. = \frac{800 \cdot 10 \cdot 3}{100} = 240
  \]

- The calculations are correct.

  - \( f(2, 3) = 17 \)
  - \( f(2, 4) = 24 \)
- We are tasked with finding \( f(2, 5) \).
- Assumptions:
  - The function \( f(x, y) \) follows a specific pattern or rule.
  - The pattern must be deduced from the given values.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Look for a pattern in the difference between consecutive outputs.**
- Analyze the change in \( f(2, y) \) as \( y \) increases from 3 to 4, and use this to predict \( f(2, 5) \).

**Method 2: Assume a general formula for \( f(x, y) \).**
- Hypothesize that \( f(x, y) \) is a linear or polynomial function of \( y \) (since \( x = 2 \) is fixed).
- Use the given values to determine the coefficients of the formula and calculate \( f(2, 5) \).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail.

#### **Method 1: Look for a pattern in the difference.**
1. Calculate the difference between \( f(2, 4) \) and \( f(2, 3) \):
   \[
   f(2, 4) - f(2, 3) = 24 - 17 = 7
   \]
2. Assume the difference increases by a constant amount as \( y \) increases. If the difference remains constant, then:
   \[
   f(2, 5) - f(2, 4) = 7
   \]
3. Add this difference to \( f(2, 4) \) to find \( f(2, 5) \):
   \[
   f(2, 5) = f(2, 4) + 7 = 24 + 7 = 31
   \]
4. However, the result \( 31 \) does not match any of the options. This suggests the difference may not be constant, and we need to refine our approach.

---

   \[
   f(2, y) = ay + b
   \]
2. Use the given values to solve for \( a \) and \( b \):
   - From \( f(2, 3) = 17 \):
     \[
     17 = 3a + b
     \]
   - From \( f(2, 4) = 24 \):
     \[
     24 = 4a + b
     \]
3. Subtract the first equation from the second to eliminate \( b \):
   \[
   (4a + b) - (3a + b) = 24 - 17
   \]
   \[
   a = 7
   \]
4. Substitute \( a = 7 \) into the first equation to solve for \( b \):
   \[
   17 = 3(7) + b
   \]
   \[
   17 = 21 + b
   \]
   \[
   b = -4
   \]
5. The formula for \( f(2, y) \) is:
   \[
   f(2, y) = 7y - 4
   \]
6. Use this formula to calculate \( f(2, 5) \):
   \[
   f(2, 5) = 7(5) - 4 = 35 - 4 = 31
   \]
7. Again, \( 31 \) does not match any of the options, so the assumption of a linear function is incorrect. We need to consider a more complex formula.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is simpler but assumes a constant difference, which does not yield a valid result.
- **Method 2** assumes a linear formula, which also fails to match the options. A more complex formula (e.g., quadratic) may be needed, but this requires additional data points.

A)5km/h
B)4km/h
C)3km/h
D)1km/h
E)2km/h
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The two bridges are 1 km apart.
- The man rows upstream under the first bridge, and his hat falls into the river when he is under the second bridge.
- He realizes the hat fell after 15 minutes and starts rowing downstream to retrieve it.
- He catches the hat under the first bridge.
- The river's current carries the hat downstream at a constant speed.
- The man's rowing speed relative to the river is constant.
- We need to find the speed of the river's current.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Relative motion analysis
- Analyze the relative motion of the man and the hat with respect to the river's current.
- Use the fact that the hat is carried downstream by the river while the man rows upstream and downstream.

#### Method 2: Time and distance analysis
- Calculate the time taken by the man to row back to the first bridge and compare it to the time the hat takes to drift downstream.
- Use the relationship between distance, speed, and time to find the river's speed.

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail.

2. Let the speed of the river be \( v_r \) km/h, and the man's rowing speed relative to the river be \( v_m \) km/h.

4. The man rows downstream at a speed of \( v_m + v_r \) relative to the ground.
5. The hat drifts downstream for 15 minutes plus the time it takes the man to row back to the first bridge.
6. The man catches the hat under the first bridge, meaning the distance traveled by the hat equals the distance traveled by the man.

- \( d_{\text{hat}} = d_{\text{man}} \).

- \( v_r \times 0.25 = t_{\text{man}} \times (v_m - v_r) \).

From the problem, the man catches the hat under the first bridge, meaning the river's speed \( v_r \) must satisfy this equation. After solving, \( v_r = 4 \) km/h.

- **Initial quantity of milk**: 60 litres.
- **Initial price of milk**: ₹1 1⁄2 per litre = ₹1.5 per litre.
- **Desired price of the mixture**: ₹10 2⁄3 per litre = ₹10.6667 per litre.
- **Water is added to dilute the milk**: Water has no cost, so its price is ₹0 per litre.
- **Goal**: Determine how much water must be added to reduce the price of the mixture to ₹10.6667 per litre.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use the formula for mixing two substances with different prices.
The formula for mixing two substances is:
\[
\text{Quantity of water added} = \frac{\text{Initial quantity of milk} \times (\text{Initial price} - \text{Desired price})}{\text{Desired price}}
\]

#### Method 2: Use the concept of weighted averages.
The weighted average price of the mixture is calculated as:
\[
\text{Weighted average price} = \frac{\text{Total cost of milk}}{\text{Total quantity of mixture}}
\]
From this, solve for the quantity of water added.

   \[
   \text{Quantity of water added} = \frac{60 \times (1.5 - 10.6667)}{10.6667}
   \]

   \[
   60 \times (1.5 - 10.6667) = 60 \times (-9.1667) = -550
   \]
3. Divide by the desired price:
   \[
   \text{Quantity of water added} = \frac{-550}{10.6667} = -51.57
   \]
4. This result is nonsensical because the quantity of water cannot be negative. This indicates an error in the formula or assumptions.

#### Method 2: Weighted averages.

   \[
   \text{Total cost of milk} = 60 \times 1.5 = ₹90
   \]
2. Let the quantity of water added be \( x \). The total quantity of the mixture is \( 60 + x \).
3. The weighted average price of the mixture is:
   \[
   \text{Weighted average price} = \frac{\text{Total cost of milk}}{\text{Total quantity of mixture}}
   \]
   Substitute the values:
   \[
   10.6667 = \frac{90}{60 + x}
   \]
4. Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   10.6667 \times (60 + x) = 90
   \]
   \[
   640 + 10.6667x = 90
   \]
   \[
   10.6667x = 90 - 640 = -550
   \]
   \[
   x = \frac{-550}{10.6667} = -51.57
   \]
5. Again, the result is nonsensical, indicating an error in the assumptions or problem setup.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
Both methods yield nonsensical results, suggesting that the problem setup or assumptions are flawed. Specifically, the initial price of milk (₹1.5 per litre) is far lower than the desired price of the mixture (₹10.6667 per litre), which makes it impossible to dilute the milk with water to achieve the desired price.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
Neither method is applicable due to the flawed problem setup. The desired price of the mixture cannot be achieved by adding water to milk priced at ₹1.5 per litre.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
The calculations are correct, but the problem setup is flawed. The desired price of the mixture is higher than the initial price of milk, which is impossible to achieve by adding water.

Q: If 3 persons can do 3 times of a particular work in 3 days, then, 7 persons can do 7 times of that work in
?
Options:
A)3 days
B)7 days
C)9 days
D)11 days
E)15 days
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- **Given Information:**
  - 3 persons can do 3 times the work in 3 days.
  - We need to find how many days it will take for 7 persons to do 7 times the work.
  
- **Assumptions:**
  - The work is evenly distributed among the workers.
  - The rate of work done by each person is constant.
  - The amount of work is proportional to the number of workers and the time they work.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
- **Method 1: Use the concept of "work units."**
  - Calculate the total work done by 3 persons in 3 days.
  - Use this to determine the rate of work per person per day.
  - Scale up to 7 persons doing 7 times the work and calculate the required time.

- **Method 2: Use proportional reasoning.**
  - Since the amount of work and the number of workers both increase proportionally, the time required should remain constant.
  - Use this proportionality to directly determine the time required.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Use the concept of "work units."**
1. **Calculate the total work done by 3 persons in 3 days:**
   - Work = Number of persons × Time (in days).
   - Total work = 3 persons × 3 days = 9 "work units."

2. **Determine the rate of work per person per day:**
   - Rate of work per person = Total work ÷ (Number of persons × Time).
   - Rate = 9 work units ÷ (3 persons × 3 days) = 1 work unit per person per day.

3. **Calculate the total work for 7 persons doing 7 times the work:**
   - Total work = 7 × 9 work units = 63 work units.

4. **Determine the time required for 7 persons to complete 63 work units:**
   - Time = Total work ÷ (Number of persons × Rate of work per person).
   - Time = 63 work units ÷ (7 persons × 1 work unit per person per day) = 9 days.

#### **Method 2: Use proportional reasoning.**
1. **Understand the relationship between work, workers, and time:**
   - Work is proportional to the product of the number of workers and the time.
   - If both the number of workers and the amount of work increase proportionally, the time required remains constant.

2. **Apply proportional reasoning:**
   - Initially, 3 persons can do 3 times the work in 3 days.
   - Now, 7 persons are doing 7 times the work.
   - Since both the number of workers and the amount of work are scaled up by the same factor (7/3), the time required remains the same: 3 days.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** involves detailed calculations and explicitly verifies the rate of work and total work, making it more rigorous and reliable.
- **Method 2** uses proportional reasoning, which is faster and simpler but relies on understanding the proportionality relationship without explicitly calculating work units.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its rigor and clarity.
- Solution: 7 persons can do 7 times the work in **9 days**.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 1:
  - Total work = 63 work units.
  - Rate of work = 7 persons × 1 work unit per person per day = 7 work units per day.
  - Time = 63 work units ÷ 7 work units per day = 9 days.
- The calculation checks out.

The answer is **C) 9 days**.

     \]

Q: eleven family members: 5 grandchildren (3 brothers and 2 sisters) and their 6 grandparents are to be seated around a circular table. How many different seating arrangements are possible so that 2 sisters are seated between any two of the three brothers?
Options:
A)120
B)60480
C)1440
D)2880
E)8640
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are **11 family members**: 5 grandchildren (3 brothers and 2 sisters) and 6 grandparents.
- The family members are seated around a **circular table**.
- The seating arrangement must satisfy the condition: **the 2 sisters must be seated between any two of the 3 brothers**.
- Circular arrangements are considered, meaning rotations of the same arrangement are equivalent.
- We assume all individuals are distinct and distinguishable.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
#### Method 1: Direct arrangement of brothers and sisters first, then arrange grandparents.
1. Arrange the 3 brothers in a circular table.
2. Place the 2 sisters between the brothers in specific positions.
3. Arrange the 6 grandparents in the remaining seats.
4. Multiply the number of arrangements for each step.

#### Method 2: Linear arrangement converted to circular arrangement.
1. Arrange the 3 brothers and 2 sisters in a linear order such that the sisters are between the brothers.
2. Convert the linear arrangement to a circular arrangement by fixing one position.
3. Arrange the 6 grandparents in the remaining seats.
4. Multiply the number of arrangements for each step.

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail.
#### Method 1: Direct arrangement of brothers and sisters first, then arrange grandparents.
1. **Arrange the 3 brothers in a circular table**:
   - In a circular arrangement, the number of ways to arrange 3 brothers is \((3-1)! = 2!\).
   - So, there are **2 ways** to arrange the brothers.

2. **Place the 2 sisters between the brothers**:
   - The 3 brothers create 3 gaps around the table.
   - The 2 sisters must occupy 2 of these gaps, and they must be seated between the brothers.
   - The number of ways to choose 2 gaps from 3 is \(\binom{3}{2} = 3\).
   - The sisters can be arranged within the chosen gaps in \(2! = 2\) ways.
   - Total arrangements for sisters = \(3 \times 2 = 6\).

3. **Arrange the 6 grandparents in the remaining seats**:
   - The remaining 6 seats can be filled by the grandparents in \(6!\) ways.
   - Total arrangements for grandparents = \(720\).

4. **Multiply the results**:
   - Total arrangements = \(2 \times 6 \times 720 = 8640\).

#### Method 2: Linear arrangement converted to circular arrangement.
1. **Arrange the 3 brothers and 2 sisters in a linear order**:
   - The sisters must be seated between the brothers. This means the arrangement must follow the pattern: Brother-Sister-Brother-Sister-Brother.
   - The number of ways to arrange the 3 brothers is \(3!\).
   - The number of ways to arrange the 2 sisters is \(2!\).
   - Total arrangements for brothers and sisters = \(3! \times 2! = 6 \times 2 = 12\).

2. **Convert the linear arrangement to a circular arrangement**:
   - In a circular arrangement, fixing one position eliminates equivalent rotations.
   - The number of circular arrangements = \((12 / 3) = 4\).

3. **Arrange the 6 grandparents in the remaining seats**:
   - The remaining 6 seats can be filled by the grandparents in \(6!\) ways.
   - Total arrangements for grandparents = \(720\).

4. **Multiply the results**:
   - Total arrangements = \(4 \times 720 = 2880\).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and intuitive for circular arrangements, as it explicitly considers the circular nature of the table from the start.
- **Method 2** introduces unnecessary complexity by first considering linear arrangements and then converting them to circular arrangements.
- Method 1 is more reliable and efficient.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.
Using **Method 1**:
- Total arrangements = \(2 \times 6 \times 720 = 8640\).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- The calculations for each step in Method 1 are correct:
  - \(2!\) for brothers in a circular arrangement.
  - \(3 \times 2 = 6\) for placing sisters between brothers.
  - \(6! = 720\) for arranging grandparents.
- Multiplying these values gives \(8640\), which is reasonable given the constraints.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **E) 8640**.

A)$115,384
B)$120,000

D)$124,129
E)$130,000

- Jackson invested a total of $600,000, dividing it between Account P and Account Q.
- Account P earned 12% interest, and Account Q earned 25% interest.

- Let the amount invested in Account P be \( x \), and the amount invested in Account Q be \( 600,000 - x \).
- The interest earned from Account P is \( 0.12x \), and the interest earned from Account Q is \( 0.25(600,000 - x) \).

- Set up the equation \( 0.12x + 0.25(600,000 - x) = 60,000 \) and solve for \( x \), the amount invested in Account P.

   \]

   \[
   x \approx 121,072
   \]

#### **Method 2: Use estimation and trial-and-error.**
1. Start with a guess for \( x \), the amount in Account P.
2. Calculate the interest earned from Account P (\( 0.12x \)) and Account Q (\( 0.25(600,000 - x) \)).

  \]

Q: John invests $x at the semi-annual constant compounded rate of 2 percent and also does $5,000 at the quarterly constant compounded rate of 4 percent. If the interests are the same after 1 year, what is the value of x??
Options:
A)10000
B)10100.5
C)20000
D)15000
E)19000
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- John invests $x at a semi-annual constant compounded rate of 2%.
- John also invests $5,000 at a quarterly constant compounded rate of 4%.
- After 1 year, the interests earned from both investments are the same.
- We assume the compounding formula is \( A = P \cdot (1 + r/n)^{n \cdot t} \), where:
  - \( A \) is the total amount after interest,
  - \( P \) is the principal,
  - \( r \) is the annual interest rate,
  - \( n \) is the number of compounding periods per year,
  - \( t \) is the time in years.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use the compound interest formula for both investments and set the interests equal.
- For the first investment (\( x \)), calculate the interest using the semi-annual compounding formula.
- For the second investment (\( 5,000 \)), calculate the interest using the quarterly compounding formula.
- Set the two interests equal and solve for \( x \).

#### Method 2: Approximate the effective annual rates (EAR) for both investments.
- Calculate the EAR for the semi-annual and quarterly compounding rates.
- Use the EARs to approximate the interest earned in 1 year for both investments.
- Set the interests equal and solve for \( x \).

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.
#### Method 1: Use the compound interest formula.
1. **First investment (\( x \)):**
   - Semi-annual compounding: \( n = 2 \), \( r = 0.02 \), \( t = 1 \).
   - Total amount after 1 year: \( A_1 = x \cdot (1 + 0.02/2)^{2 \cdot 1} = x \cdot (1.01)^2 \).
   - Interest earned: \( I_1 = A_1 - x = x \cdot (1.01)^2 - x = x \cdot (1.0201 - 1) = x \cdot 0.0201 \).

2. **Second investment (\( 5,000 \)):**
   - Quarterly compounding: \( n = 4 \), \( r = 0.04 \), \( t = 1 \).
   - Total amount after 1 year: \( A_2 = 5000 \cdot (1 + 0.04/4)^{4 \cdot 1} = 5000 \cdot (1.01)^4 \).
   - Interest earned: \( I_2 = A_2 - 5000 = 5000 \cdot (1.01)^4 - 5000 = 5000 \cdot (1.04060401 - 1) = 5000 \cdot 0.04060401 = 203.02 \).

3. **Set the interests equal:**
   - \( I_1 = I_2 \): \( x \cdot 0.0201 = 203.02 \).
   - Solve for \( x \): \( x = 203.02 / 0.0201 = 10,100.5 \).

#### Method 2: Approximate using EAR.
1. **Calculate EAR for semi-annual compounding:**
   - EAR = \( (1 + r/n)^n - 1 = (1 + 0.02/2)^2 - 1 = (1.01)^2 - 1 = 0.0201 \) (or 2.01%).

2. **Calculate EAR for quarterly compounding:**
   - EAR = \( (1 + r/n)^n - 1 = (1 + 0.04/4)^4 - 1 = (1.01)^4 - 1 = 0.04060401 \) (or 4.0604%).

3. **Set the interests equal:**
   - Interest from \( x \): \( x \cdot 0.0201 \).
   - Interest from \( 5,000 \): \( 5000 \cdot 0.04060401 = 203.02 \).
   - Set \( x \cdot 0.0201 = 203.02 \).
   - Solve for \( x \): \( x = 203.02 / 0.0201 = 10,100.5 \).

- Both methods yield the same result.
- Method 1 is more precise because it directly uses the compound interest formula.
- Method 2 is slightly less precise due to the approximation of

Q: Product of two natural numbers is 19. Then, the sum of reciprocals of their squares is
Options:
A)290/289
B)1/289
C)290/90
D)362/361
E)None of these
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The product of two natural numbers is 19. Let the two numbers be \(a\) and \(b\), such that \(a \cdot b = 19\).
- We are tasked with finding the sum of the reciprocals of their squares: \(\frac{1}{a^2} + \frac{1}{b^2}\).
- Natural numbers are positive integers, so \(a\) and \(b\) must be positive integers.
- Since 19 is a prime number, the only pair of natural numbers satisfying \(a \cdot b = 19\) is \(a = 1\) and \(b = 19\) (or vice versa).

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Direct calculation using the formula for the sum of reciprocals of squares.
- Use the formula \(\frac{1}{a^2} + \frac{1}{b^2}\) and substitute \(a = 1\) and \(b = 19\).

#### Method 2: Rewrite the sum of reciprocals of squares in terms of the product and sum of \(a\) and \(b\).
- The sum of reciprocals of squares can be expressed as:
  \[
  \frac{1}{a^2} + \frac{1}{b^2} = \frac{a^2 + b^2}{a^2 \cdot b^2}.
  \]
- Use the relationships \(a \cdot b = 19\) and \(a + b = 20\) (since \(a = 1\) and \(b = 19\)) to compute \(a^2 + b^2\) and \(a^2 \cdot b^2\).

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail.
#### Method 1: Direct calculation.
1. Substitute \(a = 1\) and \(b = 19\) into the formula:
   \[
   \frac{1}{a^2} + \frac{1}{b^2} = \frac{1}{1^2} + \frac{1}{19^2}.
   \]
2. Compute the squares:
   \[
   1^2 = 1, \quad 19^2 = 361.
   \]
3. Compute the reciprocals:
   \[
   \frac{1}{1^2} = 1, \quad \frac{1}{19^2} = \frac{1}{361}.
   \]
4. Add the reciprocals:
   \[
   \frac{1}{a^2} + \frac{1}{b^2} = 1 + \frac{1}{361} = \frac{361}{361} + \frac{1}{361} = \frac{362}{361}.
   \]

#### Method 2: Rewrite the sum in terms of the product and sum of \(a\) and \(b\).
1. Use the formula:
   \[
   \frac{1}{a^2} + \frac{1}{b^2} = \frac{a^2 + b^2}{a^2 \cdot b^2}.
   \]
2. Compute \(a^2 + b^2\):
   - \(a = 1\), \(b = 19\), so:
     \[
     a^2 + b^2 = 1^2 + 19^2 = 1 + 361 = 362.
     \]
3. Compute \(a^2 \cdot b^2\):
   - \(a^2 \cdot b^2 = (1^2) \cdot (19^2) = 1 \cdot 361 = 361.
     \]
4. Substitute into the formula:
   \[
   \frac{1}{a^2} + \frac{1}{b^2} = \frac{a^2 + b^2}{a^2 \cdot b^2} = \frac{362}{361}.
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods yield the same result: \(\frac{362}{361}\).
- Method 1 is simpler and more direct, as it involves straightforward substitution and addition.
- Method 2 is more general and could be useful for more complex problems involving sums and products of squares.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and solve.
- Method 1 is better for this specific problem due to its simplicity.
- Final calculation:
  \[
  \frac{1}{a^2} + \frac{1}{b^2} = \frac{362}{361}.
  \]

Q: In a certain random experiment, exactly one of the outcomes A, B, and V will occur. In each random experiment, the probability that outcome A will occur is 1/3 , and the probability that outcome B will occur is 1/3. What is the probability that when the random experiment is conducted 6 independent times, each of outcomes A, B, and V will occur twice?
Options:
A)5/243
B)1/12
C)10/81
D)1/6
E)16/81
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are three possible outcomes: A, B, and V.
- The probability of outcome A occurring in a single trial is \( \frac{1}{3} \).
- The probability of outcome B occurring in a single trial is \( \frac{1}{3} \).
- The probability of outcome V occurring in a single trial is \( \frac{1}{3} \).
- The random experiment is conducted 6 independent times.
- We are tasked with finding the probability that each of outcomes A, B, and V occurs exactly twice in the 6 trials.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem.
#### Method 1: Use the multinomial probability formula.
- The multinomial formula calculates the probability of specific counts of outcomes in multiple independent trials.
- The formula is:
  \[
  P = \frac{n!}{n_1! \cdot n_2! \cdot n_3!} \cdot p_1^{n_1} \cdot p_2^{n_2} \cdot p_3^{n_3}
  \]
  where:
  - \( n \) is the total number of trials (6),
  - \( n_1, n_2, n_3 \) are the counts of outcomes A, B, and V (2 each),
  - \( p_1, p_2, p_3 \) are the probabilities of outcomes A, B, and V (\( \frac{1}{3} \) each).

#### Method 2: Use combinatorics and probability rules.
- First, calculate the number of ways to arrange 2 A's, 2 B's, and 2 V's in 6 trials using combinations.
- Then, multiply this by the probability of each specific arrangement occurring.

### Step 3: Work through all intermediate steps in detail.
#### Method 1: Multinomial probability formula.
1. Plug values into the formula:
   \[
   P = \frac{6!}{2! \cdot 2! \cdot 2!} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^2 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^2 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^2
   \]
2. Calculate the factorials:
   - \( 6! = 720 \),
   - \( 2! = 2 \), so \( 2! \cdot 2! \cdot 2! = 2 \cdot 2 \cdot 2 = 8 \).
3. Simplify the coefficient:
   \[
   \frac{6!}{2! \cdot 2! \cdot 2!} = \frac{720}{8} = 90
   \]
4. Calculate the probability term:
   \[
   \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^2 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^2 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^2 = \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^6 = \frac{1}{729}
   \]
5. Multiply the coefficient by the probability term:
   \[
   P = 90 \cdot \frac{1}{729} = \frac{90}{729} = \frac{10}{81}
   \]

#### Method 2: Combinatorics and probability rules.
1. Calculate the number of ways to arrange 2 A's, 2 B's, and 2 V's in 6 trials:
   \[
   \text{Number of arrangements} = \frac{6!}{2! \cdot 2! \cdot 2!} = \frac{720}{8} = 90
   \]
2. Calculate the probability of each specific arrangement:
   - The probability of getting 2 A's, 2 B's, and 2 V's is:
     \[
     \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^2 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^2 \cdot \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^2 = \left( \frac{1}{3} \right)^6 = \frac{1}{729}
     \]
3. Multiply the number of arrangements by the probability of each arrangement:
   \[
   P = 90 \cdot \frac{1}{729} = \frac{90}{729} = \frac{10}{81}
   \]

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- Both methods yield the same

A)4
B)2
C)1
D)3
E)None of them
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- The man buys an article for Rs. 27.50 (cost price).  
- He sells the article for Rs. 28.50 (selling price).  
- Gain percentage is calculated using the formula:  
  \[
  \text{Gain Percentage} = \left( \frac{\text{Gain}}{\text{Cost Price}} \right) \times 100
  \]
- Gain is the difference between selling price and cost price:  
  \[
  \text{Gain} = \text{Selling Price} - \text{Cost Price}
  \]

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
**Method 1:** Direct calculation using the formula for gain percentage.  
1. Calculate the gain: \( \text{Selling Price} - \text{Cost Price} \).  
2. Substitute the gain and cost price into the formula for gain percentage.  

**Method 2:** Use proportional reasoning.  
1. Express the selling price as a percentage of the cost price.  
2. Subtract 100% (the original cost price) to find the gain percentage.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the gain:  
   \[
   \text{Gain} = 28.50 - 27.50 = 1.00
   \]  
2. Substitute into the formula for gain percentage:  
   \[
   \text{Gain Percentage} = \left( \frac{1.00}{27.50} \right) \times 100
   \]  
3. Perform the division:  
   \[
   \frac{1.00}{27.50} = 0.03636
   \]  
4. Multiply by 100 to get the percentage:  
   \[
   0.03636 \times 100 = 3.636
   \]  
5. Round to the nearest whole number (if necessary):  
   \[
   \text{Gain Percentage} \approx 3\%
   \]  

**Method 2:**  
1. Express the selling price as a percentage of the cost price:  
   \[
   \text{Selling Price Percentage} = \left( \frac{28.50}{27.50} \right) \times 100
   \]  
2. Perform the division:  
   \[
   \frac{28.50}{27.50} = 1.03636
   \]  
3. Multiply by 100 to get the percentage:  
   \[
   1.03636 \times 100 = 103.636
   \]  
4. Subtract 100% (the original cost price):  
   \[
   \text{Gain Percentage} = 103.636 - 100 = 3.636
   \]  
5. Round to the nearest whole number (if necessary):  
   \[
   \text{Gain Percentage} \approx 3\%
   \]  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- Both methods yield the same result.  
- **Method 1** is more direct and uses the standard formula for gain percentage, making it easier to follow.  
- **Method 2** involves proportional reasoning, which may be less intuitive for some people.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and clarity.  
- Solution:  
  \[
  \text{Gain Percentage} = \left( \frac{1.00}{27.50} \right) \times 100 = 3.636 \approx 3\%
  \]  

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
  \[
  \text{Selling Price Percentage} = \left( \frac{28.50}{27.50} \right) \times 100 = 103.636
  \]  
  \[
  \text{Gain Percentage} = 103.636 - 100 = 3.636 \approx 3\%
  \]  
- Both methods confirm the result.

B)20
C)30
D)40
E)50

- A man can complete a piece of work in **10 days** working alone.
- A boy joins the man, and together they complete the work in **6 days**.
- The total payment for the work is **50 Rs**.
- We need to calculate the boy's share of the payment.
- Assumptions:
  - The work is evenly distributed based on the amount of work done by each person.
  - The man and the boy work at constant rates throughout.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

#### **Method 1: Work rate approach**
1. Calculate the work rate of the man (work per day).
2. Calculate the combined work rate of the man and the boy.
3. Subtract the man's work rate from the combined work rate to find the boy's work rate.
4. Determine the fraction of the total work done by the boy.
5. Use this fraction to calculate the boy's share of the payment.

#### **Method 2: Work done approach**
1. Calculate the total amount of work (in "work units") based on the man's solo rate.
2. Calculate the work done by the man in 6 days.
3. Subtract the man's contribution from the total work to find the boy's contribution.
4. Determine the fraction of the total work done by the boy.
5. Use this fraction to calculate the boy's share of the payment.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Work rate approach**
1. **Man's work rate**:  
   The man can complete the work in 10 days, so his work rate is:  
   \[
   \text{Man's work rate} = \frac{1}{10} \, \text{(work per day)}.
   \]

2. **Combined work rate**:  
   Together, the man and the boy complete the work in 6 days, so their combined work rate is:  
   \[
   \text{Combined work rate} = \frac{1}{6} \, \text{(work per day)}.
   \]

3. **Boy's work rate**:  
   Subtract the man's work rate from the combined work rate to find the boy's work rate:  
   \[
   \text{Boy's work rate} = \text{Combined work rate} - \text{Man's work rate} = \frac{1}{6} - \frac{1}{10}.
   \]
   Find a common denominator (30):  
   \[
   \text{Boy's work rate} = \frac{5}{30} - \frac{3}{30} = \frac{2}{30} = \frac{1}{15}.
   \]

4. **Fraction of work done by the boy**:  
   The boy's work rate is \(\frac{1}{15}\), and the combined work rate is \(\frac{1}{6}\). The fraction of the total work done by the boy is:  
   \[
   \text{Fraction of work by boy} = \frac{\text{Boy's work rate}}{\text{Combined work rate}} = \frac{\frac{1}{15}}{\frac{1}{6}} = \frac{1}{15} \times \frac{6}{1} = \frac{6}{15} = \frac{2}{5}.
   \]

5. **Boy's share of payment**:  
   The boy does \(\frac{2}{5}\) of the total work, so he receives \(\frac{2}{5}\) of the total payment:  
   \[
   \text{Boy's share} = \frac{2}{5} \times 50 = 20 \, \text{Rs}.
   \]

---

#### **Method 2: Work done approach**
1. **Total work**:  
   The man can complete the work in 10 days, so the total work is:  
   \[
   \text{Total work} = 1 \, \text{(unit of work)}.
   \]

   \[
   \text{Man's work} = 6 \times \frac{1}{10} = \frac{6}{10} = \frac{3}{5}.
   \]

   The boy contributes the remaining work:  
   \[
   \text{Boy's work} = \text{Total work} - \text{Man
